Current Status and Future Prospects of Metal-Sulfur Batteries

Sheng-Heng Chung and Arumugam Manthiram*

Dr. S.-H. Chung, Prof. A. Manthiram Materials Science and Engineering Program & Texas Materials Institute University of Texas at Austin, Austin, Texas 78712, United States E-mail: manth@austin.utexas.edu

Keywords: lithium-sulfur batteries, metal-sulfur batteries, battery chemistry, cell fabrication, electrochemistry

Lithium-sulfur batteries have been a major focus of academic and industrial energy-storage research due to their high theoretical energy density and the use of low-cost materials. The high energy density results from the conversion mechanism that lithium-sulfur cells utilize. The sulfur cathode, being naturally abundant and environmentally friendly, makes lithium-sulfur batteries a potential nextgeneration energy-storage technology. The current state of the research indicates that we are now at the point of transitioning lithium-sulfur cells from laboratory-scale devices to a more practical energy storage application. Based on similar electrochemical conversion reactions, the low-cost sulfur cathode can be coupled with a wide range of metallic anodes, such as sodium, potassium, magnesium, calcium, and aluminum. These new “metal-sulfur” systems exhibit great potential in either lowering the production cost or producing high energy density. Inspired by the rapid development of lithium-sulfur

batteries and the prospect of metal-sulfur cells, this Progress Report summarizes over 450 research articles to analyze the research progress and explore the electrochemical characteristics, cell-assembly parameters, cell-testing conditions, and materials design. In addition to highlighting the current research progress, we also discuss the possible future areas of research that are needed to bring conversion-type lithium-sulfur and other metal-sulfur batteries into the market.

1. Introduction The ever-increasing demand for power sources with high energy density and long life has led the research community to the development of advanced energy-storage systems. The commercialization of rechargeable lithium-ion batteries is one of the great successes of modern energy-storage technology.[1-3] Due to the good reliability of lithium-ion batteries and the mature techniques for battery-assembly, the lithium-ion technology currently dominates the market for portable energy storage.[4-6] Although the current energy density of lithium-ion cells can reach around 200 W h kg−1 via insertion chemistry that makes use of intercalation materials as the electrodes (Figure 1), applications involving electric vehicles and smart grids may require rechargeable batteries with new battery chemistries that can generate even higher energy densities for longer driving ranges and higher energy-storage capabilities.[6-8] Additionally, the lithiated transition-metal oxides used in commercial lithium-ion battery cathodes tend to be expensive, scarce, or toxic to the environment. Thus, it is desirable to explore new electrode materials that are naturally abundant (and therefore less expensive) as well as environmentally compatible in order to successfully enter the future battery markets.[6,8-11] This driving force has promoted the development of batteries with conversion-reaction electrodes, in which reversible electrochemical reactions take place and new chemical compounds

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 3

form during the cycling of the battery. Naturally abundant materials, such as sulfur for the cathode, can be applied as electrodes. Different metallic anodes have shown great potential in coupling with sulfur cathode to generate a high energy density, including lithium metal[3,10,12-17,31] as well as other alkali[18-24,31] and high-valent metals.[18,25-31] As a next-generation energy-storage technology beyond lithium-ion batteries, lithium-sulfur batteries are the most promising low-cost, high-capacity energy-storage device available due to their high charge-storage capacity, low cost, and the wide availability of sulfur.[32-35] The electrochemical reactions of lithium-sulfur batteries involve reversible conversions between sulfur (S8), lithium polysulfides (Li2Sx, x = 4 - 8), and lithium sulfides (Li2S2 and Li2S).[33-36] The conversions between sulfur and lithium polysulfides and lithium sulfides involve phase changes between liquid-state polysulfides and solid-state sulfur and sulfides.[35-40] Thus, the conversion reaction of lithium-sulfur battery chemistry has no restriction in maintaining the initial crystal chemistry of the electrode during electrochemical cycling.[39-42] Therefore, a full two-electron redox reaction per sulfur atom can occur in a manner that is both reversible and stable, increasing the charge-storage capacity drastically as compared to the currently used insertion-reaction oxide cathodes.[7,39-42] As a result, sulfur cathodes possess a high theoretical charge-storage capacity of 1,672 mA h g-1, which is the highest value among solid-state cathode materials for rechargeable lithium batteries and an order of magnitude higher than that of state-of-the-art lithium-ion battery cathodes. Besides offering a high chargestorage capacity, sulfur is abundant in the crust of the Earth so that the price of sulfur ($ 150 ton-1) is almost two orders of magnitude lower than that of LiCoO2 ($ 10,000 ton-1) — the most common commercial lithium-ion battery cathode.[8,11,31,44-46] As a result, lithium-sulfur batteries have been

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 4

viewed as one of the most promising high-energy-density battery systems for the future battery market. However, the use of lithium-metal anode may soon be a problem due to the limited availability of lithium sources and the expected increase in price.[3,18-20,31] Based on the development and progress achieved with lithium-sulfur batteries,[10,12-17] researchers have explored a broad range of conversion-type battery electrochemistries coupling the high-capacity sulfur cathode with other metallic anodes, such as sodium,[18-21,31] potassium,[22-24,31] magnesium,[18,25-28,31] calcium,[18,31] and aluminum.[18,29-31] Various room-temperature metal-sulfur batteries have attracted extensive interest because of their advantages, which are very similar to lithium-sulfur batteries, including high theoretical capacity, high elemental abundance, and low cost.[14,15,18,31] However, metal-sulfur batteries also face similar challenges as lithium-sulfur cells in regards to the stability of sulfur as well as in achieving a high electrochemical utilization and stability.[47-49] During cycling, the generated discharge and charge intermediates (i.e., polysulfides) encounter unstable cyclability and corrosion of the metallic anodes. The large ionic size and high valence of other metallic anodes further slow the already sluggish reaction kinetics with sulfur cathodes.[18-22,25,29-31] Moreover, the compatibility between the electrode and the electrolyte is a key challenge in these newly-emerging metal-sulfur systems.[16,18,50-55] This Progress Report explores the current research progress in lithium-sulfur cells in terms of addressing intrinsic materials problems (e.g., the insulating nature of sulfur, irreversible relocation of polysulfides, degradation of the electrodes, and consumption of the electrolyte/electrodes) and the extrinsic cell-assembly issues (e.g., the amount of sulfur, electrolyte volume, cell-testing conditions, and concerns of cost and safety).[12-17,32-35] Then, the development and research trends in other

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 5

metal-sulfur systems, including sodium-,[18-21,31] potassium-,[18,21-24,31] magnesium-,[25-28,31] calcium-,[31] and aluminum-sulfur cells, are discussed.[18,29-31]. From recent publications on lithium-sulfur technology to other metal-sulfur systems, this Progress Report collects and summarizes detailed electrochemical characteristics, cell-assembly parameters, cell-testing conditions, and materials designs (Table 1,2). All information and data are analyzed and plotted to demonstrate the research progress in this field rather than simply reporting each case in detail or reviewing the strategies in optimizing the cell performance only.

2. Lithium-Sulfur Cells Commercial lithium-ion batteries have a graphite anode that offers a specific capacity of about 350 mA h g-1 coupled with various oxide cathodes with specific capacities of 150 – 200 mA h g1 [1-3,10]

.

During cell operation, lithium ions reversibly intercalate between the two electrodes. Based

on this insertion chemistry, lithium-ion batteries can provide a stable energy density of 150 – 200 W h kg-1.[1-3,10-12] The capacities of advanced insertion-compound cathode and anode materials could further lead to 200 – 300 W h kg-1 and even approach 350 W h kg-1 using low-cobalt- or high-nickelcontent cathodes coupled with carbon/silicon composite anodes.[1-3,11] However, commercial electrode materials will soon reach their ceiling in capacity values due to the limited crystallographic sites naturally available in these compounds for the insertion and extraction of lithium ions. Moreover, in order to maintain the crystal structure of insertion-compound cathodes, lithium-ion cells may not fully utilize their charge-storage capacity.[10-12] Due to the limitations of lithium-ion batteries, many research groups are exploring new battery chemistries that store energy through conversion reactions between electrode materials

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 6

that feature high charge-storage capacities. For example, using lithium metal as the anode, lithiumsulfur and lithium-air batteries have been developed, which have porous cathode substrates that hold, respectively, sulfur and oxygen.[6-8,32-35] Both the anode and cathode materials generate electricity through conversion reactions, bypassing the need to maintain the crystal structure and thus enabling the delivery of high energy densities that can exceed 500 W h kg-1 (Figure 1).[1-3,10-12] In lithium-sulfur cells, lithium metal is used as the anode and sulfur as the cathode. Having lithium metal as the anode material provides a high theoretical charge-storage capacity of 3,862 mA h g-1 and a low reduction potential of -3.04 V (Li/Li+) v.s. SHE.[56-58] Moreover, unlike graphite anodes in commercial lithium-ion batteries, lithium metal by itself can function as the anode without the need for a host structure that requires conductive carbon and binder. Using pure lithium metal as the anode also avoids the need for a porosity of at least 20% in order for the electrolyte to access the active material through the conventional lithium-ion battery electrode.[7,56-58] These unique features of lithium-sulfur cells enable high energy density values (Figure 1).[10-12] On the other side of lithium-sulfur batteries, the sulfur cathode has a high charge-storage capacity of 1,672 mA h g-1 on coupling with lithium-metal anode, which generates high theoretical gravimetric and volumetric energy densities of, respectively, 2,500 W h kg-1 and 2,780 W h L-1. Such high energy densities outperform commercial lithium-ion cells, which currently offer theoretical gravimetric and volumetric energy densities of just 365 W h kg-1 and 1,370 W h L-1.[10,47] Because of these advantages, lithium-sulfur cells are already becoming commercially viable in some niche applications.[14,58,59] For example, Sion Power has installed lithium-sulfur batteries providing an energy density of 350 W h kg-1 in an unmanned aircraft system. In 2015, OXIS Energy developed prototype lithium-sulfur batteries with energy densities that ranged from 350 to 400 W h

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 7

kg-1, which are currently operating in small-scale commercial and test applications. In 2018, OXIS announced the use of their 10 Ah Long Life cells in electric bikes. Other companies are rushing to develop sulfur batteries as well. Sony, which commercialized the first lithium-ion batteries, plans to introduce lithium-sulfur batteries to the market in 2020.[58-65] In spite of this progress in lithium-sulfur technology, the research community has also witnessed bottlenecks in mass production and fierce competition with established commercial lithium-ion batteries.[46,47,66-70] Lithium-sulfur cells are challenged with several intrinsic material characteristics, including the insulating nature of sulfur, the irreversible relocation of polysulfides, the volume change of sulfur cathode, and the degradation of lithium-metal anode.[10,46,57,71] In addition to the intrinsic material characteristics, lithium-sulfur research has also encountered extrinsic challenges blocking the technology’s future development. For example, the high performance of lithium-sulfur cells reported in the literature is often achieved using idealized conditions. Conditions that cause the electrochemical characteristics of lithium sulfur cells to be overrated include the use of an insufficient sulfur loading and content of < 4 mg cm -2[72-75] and < 60 wt.%, [72-76] respectively, as well as an excess electrolyte/sulfur ratio of > 11 μL mg-1[77-81] and excessive lithium metal. This unfortunately results in an overestimated electrochemical performance in terms of sulfur utilization, effective capacities, and cell cyclability. Furthermore, the mass contributed from the aluminum-metal current collector (~ 5 mg cm-2) should be included in the calculations of the effective capacities and energy density of the device, but often is not.[12,46,58,59,66] In addition, although we have not yet found effective solutions to the intrinsic and extrinsic problems of the cathode mentioned above, it is known in the research community that most progress in the literature is based on the cathode material only rather than taking a balanced

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 8

approach to the device. Thus, more efforts are needed to explore the electrolyte (including the formula, salts, and additives) and the metallic anode in parallel rather than focusing too much on improving individual components and/or electrochemical characteristics.[46,58,59]

2.1. Lithium-Sulfur Electrochemistry The typical configuration of a lithium-sulfur battery (Figure 2a) is assembled using a thin piece of lithium metal as the anode and a porous cathode substrate with polymeric binder to host the sulfur.[35-37] A separator is configured between the two electrodes with an appropriate amount of electrolyte added.[38-42,82] Lithium metal is oxidized during cell discharging. The generated lithium ions and electrons transport from the anode to the sulfur cathode through, respectively, the internal electrolyte and the external electrical circuit. After receiving the lithium ions and electrons, the sulfur cathode is reduced to lithium sulfides. When charging the cell, the formed lithium sulfides convert back to sulfur and the lithium ions return to the lithium-metal anode. The overall reversible redox reactions are S + 2Li = Li2S (e.g., S8 + 16Li = 8 Li2S with element S8) with 2Li = 2Li+ + 2e- (e.g., 16Li = 16Li+ + 16e-) at the anode side and S + 2e- = S2- (e.g., S8 + 16e- = 8 S2- with element S8) at the cathode side.[37,82-85] The redox reactions between solid-state sulfur and lithium sulfides undergo compositional and structural conversions. These involve the formation of lithium polysulfides, a group of dissolved intermediates with the chemical formula of Li2Sx. The value of x can range from 4 to 8.[35-37] During the discharge process, two plateaus are seen in the discharge curve (Figure 2b), along with the gradual decrease in the sulfur chain length and the phase transition from solid-state sulfur to dissolved polysulfides and then to solid-state sulfides.[32,35-37] The upper discharge plateau typically

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 9

occurs at 2.15 – 2.4 V, which indicates the reduction of sulfur to Li2S8 and subsequently to Li2S6 and Li2S4 (Figure 2b). This conversion step features 0.5 electron transfer per sulfur atom and so corresponds to a theoretical capacity of 418 mA h g-1.[35,86-90] The generated lithium polysulfides have a high solubility in the ether-based liquid electrolyte predominately used in current lithium-sulfur research. The dissolved polysulfides have high electrochemical activity and mobility, functioning as a catholyte and enabling fast reaction kinetics and enhanced electrochemical utilization of the sulfur cathode.[38,91,92] Polysulfide dissolution also increases the electrolyte viscosity and decreases the overall cell voltage.[9,13,72,93-96] Thus, as the discharge proceeds, the lower discharge plateau typically occurs at 2.1 V, which corresponds to the reduction of lithium polysulfides with the incorporation of extra lithium ions toward the precipitation of highly insoluble and insulating lithium sulfides (Li2S2 and Li2S) (Figure 2b). 1.5 electrons transfer per sulfur atom during this liquid-to-solid phase transition, which leads to a theoretical capacity of 1,254 mA h g-1.[35,86] The solid-to-solid conversion between Li2S2 and Li2S involve sluggish reaction kinetics.[49,73,97-99] Moreover, the sulfide deposited on the electrode surface impedes the access of lithium ions and electrons. Therefore, the end-discharge products may not totally convert back to elemental sulfur. As a result, the realistic discharge capacity of lithium-sulfur cells is lower than the theoretical value.[98-101]

2.2. Lithium-Sulfur Electrochemical Performance In this next section we discuss the electrochemical performance of lithium-sulfur cells in terms of the intrinsic and extrinsic problems that are associated with, respectively, the material characteristics as well as cell-assembly (e.g., sulfur content, loading, and mass, amount of electrolyte, etc.) and testing conditions (e.g., rate and voltage) (Figure 3).[12,45-47,58,59] These problems

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 10

are also either currently a challenge in other metal-sulfur systems or are expected to emerge very soon.[18-30,58,59] This is because both lithium- and other metal-sulfur cells have insulating sulfur as the cathode material, generate high charge-storage capacity by a complex conversion reaction, and have a sensitive battery chemistry toward any one of the above-mentioned extrinsic problems.[7,45-47]

2.2.1. Insulating Nature of Sulfur The combination of a lithium anode with a sulfur cathode gives the highest charge-storage capacity among solid cathode materials for rechargeable lithium batteries. However, both sulfur and its end-discharge product Li2S are insulating, having low electronic and ionic conductivities of ~ 10-30 S cm-2 and ~ 10-14 S cm-2, respectively.[37,45-47] The high cathode resistance directly constrains the efficient electrochemical reactions and results in a low utilization of the active material. The resulting sluggish reaction kinetics has been reported to cause incomplete conversion reactions in the cell. Thus, unreacted sulfur clusters can still exist in the cathode after repeated cycling.[102-105] The observation of the remaining unreacted sulfur in a cycled cathode also implies that fast solid-toliquid reaction kinetics is not enough to achieve the full utilization of the upper-plateau discharge capacity. Additionally, such high resistance brings additional challenges to the solid-solid conversion during the lower discharge plateau.[35,105,106] The limited kinetics for both electrons and lithium ions results in a higher polarization in the lower discharge plateau voltage and a limited lower-plateau discharge capacity.[36,106a,106b] Thus, as we consider the ratio of the capacities contributed from the lower discharge plateau to that from the upper discharge, the obtained value is usually less than the theoretical value of 3.[106] Moreover, during repeated cycling, the continuous precipitation of

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 11

insulating lithium sulfides on the surface of electrodes also creates a passive coating, which increases the overpotential and limits the steady high capacity output.[12,98-101] In order to attain a high and stable electrochemical utilization of sulfur, a uniform dispersion of insulating sulfur in the conductive cathode substrate is required. Good electrical contact with sufficient conductive additives might be necessary to facilitate a smooth conversion of sulfur.[12,102105]

A further improved redox kinetics of sulfur cathode could be realized by applying various

electrocatalysts (e.g., metals,[107a,107b,108] metallic compounds,[107c-107e,109] and polar materials[108,109b]) in the sulfur cathode as redox mediators. These mediators possess high catalytic activity, which enable sulfur to pexhibit enhanced conversion efficiency and stability.[108] In consideration of the insulating nature of sulfur, a high content and mass loading of a conductive matrix and additional auxiliary electrocatalysts may be needed. However, a low sulfur content resulted would cause the cell to have a low effective capacity and energy density.[86,98,108-111]

2.2.2. Irreversible Relocation of Polysulfides Liquid-state lithium polysulfides that are generated as intermediates during the conversion reaction are essential for achieving a uniform dispersion of sulfur and enhanced reaction kinetics.[17,79,91,105] Lithium polysulfides are highly soluble in the most common organic electrolytes used in lithium-sulfur research. The dissolved polysulfides in the liquid electrolyte possess good mobility and strong electrochemical activity. The relocation of dissolved polysulfides within the cathode region assists in the uniform dispersion of the active material[91,112-114] and avoids the formation of solid sulfur clusters.[105,115,116] In addition, the dissolved polysulfides function as a redox mediator to facilitate the redox reactions and also activates the remaining sulfur embedded within

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 12

the cathode structure for subsequent reactions.[91,103,117] These positive features of polysulfides make the active material easy to access for the lithium ions in the electrolyte solution and electrons from the conductive cathode substrate, which increases the electrochemical utilization and rate capability. Therefore, the dissolution of polysulfides and their formation as the catholyte in the cathode region are essential for realizing the current aim of making high-loading sulfur cathodes with sufficiently high capacities and long cyclability.[17,45-47] However, the high mobility and strong electrochemical activity of dissolved polysulfides also enables them to easily and irreversibly diffuse out from the cathode region. Uncontrolled polysulfide diffusion can impact the electrochemical stability of the cells, resulting in a fast capacity fade of over 50% in a short 50 cycles.[92,118-120] Without suitable cathode fabrication and/or cathode configuration, the dissolved polysulfides that generate and accumulate in the cathode region of the cell will migrate from the cathode toward the anode due to the concentration gradient formed.[12,119-121] The migrating polysulfides are reduced by lithium to form insoluble lithium sulfides via a disproportionation reaction, which causes the loss of active material and anode passivation.[45-47,121] Upon charging, some migrating polysulfides are reduced by the lithium-metal anode, which forms short-chain polysulfides that shuttle back to the cathode because of the electric field from the anode to the cathode.[12,45,118-121 The resulting polysulfide shuttle is known to cause the low electrochemical efficiency of lithium-sulfur cells.[12,45,122-123] During repeated cycling, the continuous dissolution, relocation, and redeposition of the active material lead to the irreversible rearrangement and inhomogeneous distribution of insulating sulfur in the cells, which creates inactive regions both in and on the electrodes. This increases the cell impedance and damages the electrode integrity, decreasing the reaction reversibility and cyclability of the cell.[118,119]

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 13

As a result, lithium polysulfides play a key role in developing lithium-sulfur cells with high energy density, while also causing the unfavorable electrochemical properties, including fast capacity fade, poor Coulombic efficiency, and short cycle life.[17,118,119] One possibility for enhancing the positive properties of polysulfides is to develop a suitable cathode configuration for hosting polysulfides within the cathode region. Currently, the research community has proposed several promising cell configurations, such as interlayers,[83,124-132] coated separators,[129,133-143] and porous current collectors.[83,144-150] Another possible route is the development of better electrolytes, such as optimizing the electrolyte solvent and/or salt,[66,151,152] application of organic electrolyte containing high salt concentration (e.g., > 1.0 M),[17,152-156] and liquid ionic electrolytes with additives.[157-162] Recently, heteroatom-doped and polar materials that possess a strong chemical interaction with lithium polysulfides have been designed for Li-S cells.[108,163] Anchoring and/or trapping the polysulfide intermediates through the lithium-bond chemistry benefits the polysulfide retention and reutilization.[163,164] Additionally, solid electrolytes should be effective at blocking the dissolution and migration of polysulfides. However, the lack of dissolved polysulfides would deteriorate the already sluggish reaction kinetics of the cell. The compatibility of the materials interfaces in a solid electrolyte battery configuration would be an added challenge.[9,16,17,165]

2.2.3. Volume Change of the Sulfur Cathode In designing the component and configuration of lithium-sulfur cells, it is necessary to address the volume changes between sulfur and lithium sulfide. Sulfur has a density of 2.07 g cm-3, while lithium sulfide has 1.66 g cm-3. Thus, the conversion reaction between them results in an active-material volume change of up to 80%.[15,32,152,166] Such a large volumetric change during cycling

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 14

leads to a pulverization of the cathode materials.[12,45-47,153] A sulfur cathode with damaged integrity causes the insulating sulfur to lose contact with the conductive agents. Without good electrical contact, the isolated sulfur loses access to electrons and becomes an inactive region of the cathode, raising the cell impedance and resulting in capacity fade.[12,84,166-168] Ideally, maintaining an appropriate void in the electrode could improve the tolerance to volume changes of the active material and maintain the cathode integration.[15,144-150] However, sulfur has an innate low volumetric energy density, and a high void cathode configuration would further diminish the volumetric energy density.[15,34,79-81] Therefore, a well-controlled balance between the porosity characteristics of the electrode and the resulting volumetric energy density needs to be achieved.[15,34,46,80]

2.2.4. Degradation of the Lithium-Metal Anode Aside from the development of a suitable sulfur cathode that addresses general challenges for both lithium-sulfur and other metal-sulfur cells, the lithium-metal anode also needs significant optimization. Lithium metal has been used in the development of next-generation rechargeable batteries with high energy densities of over 400 W h Kg-1 due to its high charge-storage capacity, strong activity, and light weight.[12-17,169-172] The strong activity allows lithium to easily react with organic electrolytes to form a solid electrolyte interphase (SEI) on the reacted lithium anode. This SEI layer passivates the lithium metal and ideally offers some electrochemical and chemical stability.[169,170,172-175] However, during cell operation, lithium ions continuously and randomly strip and plate on the lithium anode, leading to the growth of high-surface-area mosses and dendrites.[169,171] Thus, the

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 15

lithium plating/striping process creates large and inhomogeneous volume changes in the lithium anode, causing it to crack and generate inactive regions.[15,171,172] The resulting inhomogeneous surface causes a nonuniform distribution of current density, particularly at the tips of the material and a lithium-ion concentration gradient at the interface between the electrode and electrolyte.[12,15,173-176] Lithium dendrites that result from these conditions can cause short circuits that raise both performance and safety concerns. Dendritic growth is a major challenge that blocks the direct use of lithium metal as a high-performance anode for coupling with high-capacity sulfur cathodes.[12,169,172-180] The combination of inhomogeneous lithium plating/stripping and dendritic growth causes the SEI layer to be unstable, with fresh lithium exposed in the cracks. These effects induce an irreversible consumption of active lithium and electrolyte during each re-formation of the SEI layer during repeated cycling.[173-176] Moreover, coupling the lithium anode with the sulfur cathode generates irreversible polysulfide migration that can form an insulating lithium-sulfide passivation layer on the cycled lithium, which intrinsically limits the charge-discharge efficiency and cycle stability of the cell.[110a,173,174] The reactions among lithium, migrating polysulfides, and organic electrolyte at the lithium-metal anode also generates gaseous byproducts in the sealed cell.[12,173,181] The formation of gases can create gaps in the compact cell components, potentially leading to a broken circuit. With even more gas, the internal pressure of the cell can increase to such an extent that it can break the sealed packaging and cause device failure.[181] Therefore, in order to bring lithium-sulfur cells and high-capacity sulfur cathodes into the battery market, we need new methods of stabilizing the lithium-metal anode with the presence of a high-loading sulfur cathode. Another

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 16

solution is the development of alternative anodes for high-capacity sulfur cathodes to address these pressing issues.[10,18,21,31]

2.3. Performance Testing and Evaluation Lithium-sulfur technology is more progressed than other metal-sulfur cells due to the intensive research efforts that have advanced it closer to practical viability.[18,58-65] Before 2010, there were less than 50 publications on lithium-sulfur batteries a year, but since 2016 that number has increased to thousands of papers every year.[7,10,46-49,59,66] The fundamental understanding established by the lithium-sulfur community has resulted in fast progress in lithium-sulfur cells, from basic scientific research to practical cell design. The intensive research on lithium-sulfur cells has established fundamental knowledge in terms of battery chemistry and intrinsic material challenges and the corresponding solutions, as we summarized in sections 2.1 and 2.2. We next summarize the progress in the cell performance and extrinsic challenges, such as the cell-assembly parameters and the conditions of cell testing and evaluation.

2.3.1. Cell Performance In early studies during 2003 – 2015,[182-191] as well as in some current work (2016 – 2018),[192202]

multiple solutions have been proposed to optimize the physicochemical properties of

nanocomposites developed to seal sulfur in composite cathodes (Table 1).[182-202] Table 1 lists different composite cathodes reported by various groups, which are categorized as: “sulfur / the nanocomposite host applied.” Most of the pioneering studies have sought to improve the discharge capacity and cycle life. Typically, these early work have shown sulfur utilization values of less than

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 17

50%, which equals a charge-storage capacity of less than 800 mA h mg-1. In addition, the cycle life of these devices is generally less than 100 cycles. The cell capacity and cycle life have made significant progress with the development of optimized nanocomposites in sulfur cathodes starting in 2008, use of LiNO3 in 2012, and application of newly-designed cell components from 2015. These engineered nanocomposites[72,203b] and modified cell components (e.g., separator and cathode structure)[15,83,129,150,204] deliver a peak chargestorage capacity of over 1,600 mA h mg-1. Long cyclabilities of over 1,000 cycles have been demonstrated with numerous, various composite cathodes,[83,166,205,206] interlayers,[12,124-132,207] porous cathode substrates,[83,144-150,208] sandwiched cathode configurations,[81,111,147,209] and coated separators.[133-136] Better electrochemical characteristics have also been realized by integrating newly-developed cell components, such as the application of sulfur/carbon nanocomposites in the cathode with either a TiO2/TiN coating[205h] or a MoSe2/reduced graphene oxide coating on the separator (Figure 4a,b).[206b] Although many cathode designs and material developments have been used to report highperformance lithium-sulfur cells, these devices have not yet reached the rechargeable battery market. Only in the last few years, the research community has started to inspect the missing gap between laboratory-scale devices to more practical, commercial batteries. According to an analysis provided by OXIS, over 60% of these lithium-sulfur battery papers have demonstrated primarily progress on the design of advanced nanocomposites to host sulfur in the composite cathode.[59] However, there are still many other problems associated with lithium-sulfur cells that have not attracted nearly as much attention, such as the amount of sulfur in the electrode,[46,59,76,90,210-212] the amount of electrolyte in the cell,[46,59,76,210-214] the cycling rate, the operation voltage,[59,210,214] the

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 18

metallic lithium anode,[37,172-175] etc. Moreover, the new findings reported in the literature show interesting results, proving that the lithium-sulfur system is very sensitive to the extrinsic experimental parameters mentioned above.[7,10,12,46] Consequently, the current progress in lithiumsulfur battery cathodes may be inadequate for a comprehensive evaluation of the lithium-sulfur technology.[7,10,12,46,58,59] In the current studies, the research community has focused on a comprehensive examination of lithium-sulfur electrochemical performance by addressing the intrinsic battery chemistry and extrinsic cell configuration and design. More and more publications are now reporting the amount of sulfur used in the cathode (Table 1).[12-16,45-51] Increasing studies further revolve around establishing high-energy-density lithium-sulfur batteries based on the design of a highloading sulfur cathode with reasonably lean electrolyte while still attaining high electrochemical utilization.[9,12,45-47,103] Reporting these necessary cell-assembly parameters benefits the research community so that we can understand whether the reported electrochemical characteristics can be attributed to a promising solution or whether they are achieved by applying idealized conditions for collecting data.

2.3.2. Cell-Assembly Parameters As an electrochemical-conversion type cell, the battery chemistry and performance of lithium-sulfur cells are significantly controlled by several important cell-assembly parameters, including the amount of sulfur in the cathode, the amount and composition of the electrolyte used, and the lithium metal in the anode.[45,46,210-214] As a result, this Progress Report will consider the relation between these cell-assembly parameters and their effect on the cell electrochemistry and

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 19

performance. In general, the amount of sulfur mainly impacts the effective capacities, including areal, gravimetric, and volumetric capacity values. The amount of sulfur and electrolyte used in the cell control the energy density of the battery.[12,46,66] The electrolyte formula and the lithium-metal anode affect the discharge/charge efficiency of the cell, which therefore control the cyclability and stability. [45-47,66,210-214]

Sulfur Loading: The areal sulfur loading is often simply referred to as “sulfur loading,” with units of mg cm-2 (Figure 4c and Table 1). This value effectively decides the areal capacity and the energy density of the device.[12,46] For a reference, in order to power an electric vehicle, we need the areal capacity of lithium-ion batteries to exceed 4 mA h cm-2.[97,215,216] Current lithium-sulfur cells have an operating voltage of 2.1 V, which is low compared to the high voltage of the lithium-ion system (3.5 V). Because of the lower voltage, a higher areal capacity of over 7 mA h cm -2 is necessary for lithiumsulfur cells to compete with state-of-the-art lithium-ion batteries.[12,46] Ideally, a desirable areal capacity should be determined by the sulfur loading, which means that increasing the sulfur loading should result in a high effective capacity. However, in real cells, high-loading sulfur cathodes suffer from low sulfur utilization due to the insulating nature of sulfur toward both ions and electrons.[12,46,103,216] In addition to the electrochemical issue, constructing high-loading sulfur cathodes has other problems. A thick cathode creates a long transfer distance for charges. The structural stability of the thick cathode itself is also a problem.[84,103,215,216] Due to these challenges, even if a high charge-storage capacity is reported using a low-loading sulfur cathode with impressive cycle stability, it does not guarantee that the cell performance can be maintained with higher areal

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 20

sulfur loadings in practical cells. Furthermore, even with a high sulfur utilization, a low-loading sulfur cathode always has poor energy density as compared to the current lithium-ion technology.[10,12,45,46] Our findings in Table 1 show that when desirable areal capacities (7 mA h cm -2) beyond lithium-ion technology were achieved, the sulfur utilization was about 60% on average. Thus, a relatively high sulfur loading of at least 7 mg cm-2 to compensate for the reduced sulfur utilization is necessary so that the cell can obtain good areal capacity. When a sulfur cathode has a high activematerial loading similar to the lithium-ion battery cathode (e.g., 10 mg cm−2 active material), most 10 mg cm−2 sulfur-loading cathodes can only utilize 50% of their charge-storage capacity.[1-3,10-15] In general, as the sulfur loading is increased, the sulfur utilization reported in the literature decreases. This implies the necessity to study and develop high-loading sulfur cathodes with a sulfur loading of over 7 mg cm-2 (ideally, 10 mg cm-2) and with better electrochemical utilization of sulfur. However, although high-loading sulfur cathodes have been reported since 2013, the average sulfur loading is typically below 4 – 5 mg cm-2 (Figure 4c). On the way to realize a high-loading sulfur cathode, we reported recently the use of carbonized cotton as a cathode material substrate to attain high sulfur loadings approaching 60 mg cm-2 with a high sulfur content of over 70 wt.%.[79,105,115]

Sulfur Content: The sulfur content should reflect the total sulfur content in the whole cathode as wt.% sulfur (Figure 4d and Table 1), which allows the estimation of the gravimetric capacity of the cell.[12,79,115,210-212] Ideally, in order to attain a high enough gravimetric capacity of at least 800 mA h g−1, high-loading sulfur cathodes require a high sulfur content of over 70 wt.%, while maintaining a reasonably high sulfur utilization of around 70%.[7,10,12,46] However, attaining such a high sufficient gravimetric capacity is facing intrinsic materials and extrinsic cell-assembly challenges.

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 21

First, although a high sulfur content of over 70 wt.% in the cathode is necessary to build lithium-sulfur batteries with a high energy density, it allows the use of a low content of conductive agents and a limited amount of substrates to trap polysulfides.[7,10,12,46] These result in high cathode resistance and fast active-material loss. The resulting low charge-storage capacity makes the cells hard to attain a high gravimetric capacity and energy density.[10,12,46] Second, most of the lithiumsulfur studies report different sulfur-based nanocomposites and individually highlight a high sulfur content of 70 – 80 wt.% based on the sulfur amount in the nanocomposites, but not in the whole cathode. However, the reported sulfur content is usually higher than the actual value due to the addition of extra conductive additives and binders.[12,111] The conductive additives (e.g., carbon back, Super

P,

acetylene

black,

etc.)

and

binders

(e.g.,

polyvinylidene

fluoride

(PVDF),

polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE), styrene-butadiene rubber / carboxymethylcellulose (SBR/CMC), etc.) can be 10 – 30 wt.% of the cathode.[12,46,111] Therefore, the research community faces a challenge in improving the sulfur content from 40 – 60 wt.% to over a desired sulfur content of 70 wt.%.[12,46,66] The challenges in improving the sulfur content are indeed reflected in the research progress (Figure 4d). The research progress shows a rapid increase in the average sulfur content from less than 40 wt.% to almost 60 wt.% in 2016. However, this trend stopped after 2016 and we now face a bottleneck in attaining 60 wt.% sulfur in the whole cathode. One possible solution to make highloading cathodes achieve a high sulfur content is to encapsulate dissolved polysulfides as the catholyte within a conductive cathode substrate to form a self-supporting / free-standing sulfur cathode.[10,15,103] Several lithium-sulfur studies have demonstrated the feasibility and reported a high sulfur content of over 70 wt.% by using carbonized cotton substrates (70 – 75 wt.% sulfur),[79,80] a

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 22

carbon fiber foam (76wt.% sulfur),[115] carbon yolk-shell nanosheets (73 wt.% sulfur),[209g] and graphene@g-C3N4 hybrid sponge (82 wt.% sulfur).[209h]

Electrode: New progress in establishing pouch-type lithium-sulfur cells from coin-type cells requires the use of large-sized electrodes.[12,46,66] A large-sized sulfur cathode contains a much higher amount of sulfur than that in a coin cell. The high sulfur mass per electrode results in a high overall inner resistance, which impacts the electrochemical utilization and stability of the resulting batteries. A typical coin cell has an areal size of ~ 1.0 cm2.[12-15,66] However, the areal size of a pouch cell is usually ~ 40 cm2. With the same sulfur loading and content in the cathode, a pouch-type cell can result in much higher total inner resistance, larger amount of diffusing polysulfides, and more nonuniform distribution of the active material.[46,215-217] Thus, the evolution of pouch-type lithium-sulfur cells encounters all pre-existing intrinsic materials challenges more significantly in addition to new extrinsic problems in cell fabrication, which cause performance differences between coin cells used in materials research and the large pouch cells used in practical applications.[12,66,215-217]

Electrolyte/Sulfur Ratio: The research progress in exploring the relationship between cell-assembly parameters and lithium-sulfur cell performance has led the research community to understand another insurmountable challenge — the need for significantly reducing the amount of electrolyte in the cell for achieving acceptable energy density. Most lithium-sulfur studies commonly mention the amount of electrolyte as the “electrolyte/sulfur ratio” or “electrolyte-to-sulfur ratio” with units of μL mg-1 (Figure 4e and Table 1). It is an important parameter in a testing cell that can influence the electrochemical performance and even the battery chemistry.[46,59,76-81,210-214] The lithium-sulfur

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 23

system goes through a reversible solid-liquid-solid phase conversion. The two solid-state charge and discharge products, sulfur and lithium sulfides, have poor electrical and ionic conductivities and are insoluble in the electrolyte. Thus, good surface wetting of sulfur and lithium sulfides is essential to ameliorate the sluggish reactions that take place at the solid-electrolyte interface. In addition, a sufficient amount of electrolyte is necessary to maintain a smooth lithium-ion transfer.[216,218-221] However, the amount of electrolyte needed is usually too high. The excess electrolyte further dilutes the concentration of the irreversibly relocated polysulfides. These result in over-rated electrochemical efficiency and stability.[12,46,66,76-79] Besides influencing the electrochemical performance of the cells, the electrolyte/sulfur ratio also plays a key role in determining the energy density of practical lithium-sulfur batteries. The electrolyte is an inactive material in the cell so that a high electrolyte/sulfur ratio can result in a low energy density.[12,15,66,76,79] Ether-based electrolyte is commonly used in lithium-sulfur research and has a density of ~ 1.0 mg μL-1. For a typical high electrolyte/sulfur ratio of 20 μL mg-1, the cell can deliver no more than 1/20th of the energy density expected based on sulfur cathode. Recent studies have reduced the electrolyte/sulfur ratio to approach ~ 10 μL mg-1 in 2017, down from ~ 50 μL mg-1 used in earlier work (Figure 4e). However, the electrochemical efficiency and reversibility of the resulting cell drops at such a low electrolyte/sulfur ratio of 10 μL mg-1. Low electrolyte/sulfur ratios often lead to reduced sulfur utilization and a low cycle life of less than 40 cycles because of insufficient wetting of the electrode and continuous electrolyte depletion during cycling.[79,80,216,220] Thus, more efforts are needed to further reduce the electrolyte/sulfur ratio to less than 5 – 6 μL mg-1 to ensure a high gravimetric energy density but without compromising electrode wetting or leading to electrolyte depletion. Possible approaches are currently reported that include the application of a

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 24

substrate or cathode with optimized nanopores that enable the cell to function at a reduced electrolyte/sulfur ratio while maintaining cycle stability.[46,59,76-81] Another effective concept to reduce the electrolyte/sulfur ratio has recently been proposed with the use of highly solvating electrolytes. The investigation into various electrolytes with high donor numbers (e.g. 1,2dimethoxyethane (DME), N,N-dimethylacetamide, dimethyl sulfoxide, and 1-methylimidazole) allows the formation of a high amount of polysulfide catholyte, which is necessary for high-loading sulfur cathodes to increase the sulfur utilization at low electrolyte conditions.[151]

Practical conditions: In current lithium-sulfur research, there is an increasing focus on balancing a high amount of sulfur in the cathode along with a reduced electrolyte/sulfur ratio in the assembled cell (Figure 4f). Figure 4f shows research trends by displaying the percentage of studies that have demonstrated desirable sulfur loadings, contents, and electrolyte/sulfur ratios until 2018. The suggested lowest standards are 5 mg cm-2 of sulfur loading, 60 wt.% of sulfur content, and 11 μL mg-1 for the electrolyte/sulfur ratio in conducting lithium-sulfur research.[12,46,66,76-79,113,114] Currently, over 30% of lithium-sulfur studies demonstrate high-performance lithium-sulfur cells using high-loading sulfur cathodes with at least 5 mg cm-2 sulfur in cathodes. On the other hand, an increasing number of publications have reported a high sulfur content of at least 60 wt.% in the cathode from 2012 to 2018. In 2017 and 2018, over 50% of lithium-sulfur studies have reported high cell performance with sulfur contents of over 60 wt%. However, compared to the amount of sulfur, decreasing the electrolyte/sulfur ratio has faced more challenges. Less than 25% of studies have been able to reduce the electrolyte/sulfur ratio to less than 11 μL mg-1. Although it is necessary to further study

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 25

all three parameters comprehensively, unfortunately, the trends in achieving high sulfur loading/content and low electrolyte/sulfur ratio decreased in 2018.[12,45-47,173] As a result of the need for investigating lithium-sulfur cells with more practical conditions, some research articles published in 2017 and 2018 have demonstrated the possibility of upgrading the critical parameters for lithium-sulfur technology to be practically viable.[12,45-47] These work have comprehensively achieved all practical cell-assembly conditions, with sulfur loadings of over 5 mg cm-2, sulfur contents of over 60 wt.%, and low electrolyte/sulfur ratios of less than 11 μL mg-1. Specifically, these promising studies have attained better sulfur loading and electrolyte/sulfur ratios of over 8 mg cm-2 and less than 4.5 μL mg-1, respectively, using nanocomposites with a hierarchically structured sulfur host and a cross-linked binder,[222] a nanocomposite with a micro-nano-structured sulfur host,[223] a nanocomposite made of a sulfur host featuring large intrinsic macropores,[224] and a ternary hierarchical nanocomposite with a fibrous carbon skeleton, porous carbon cube, and carbon nanotubes.[225] However, these cases with sulfur-based nanocomposites coated on aluminum-foil current collectors commonly show a reduced cycle life of 10 – 20 cycles.[222-224] Long cycle stability of up to 200 cycles was achieved by directly applying an interpenetrating metal-organic framework/carbon nanotube thin film[226] and a nonporous carbon nanofiber substrate with optimized low microporosity[111] as the self-supporting cathode substrate.[10,15,103] In addition to the design of cathode substrates for sulfur cathodes, organosulfide/organopolysulfide electrochemical couples have recently been reported as promising cathode materials. The sulfurcontaining polymer-based active materials with low-molecular-weight organic groups reduce the formation of soluble long-chain polysulfide intermediates, achieving a high sulfur content of approaching 90 wt.% and exhibiting stable cyclability at a lean electrolyte condition of less than 7 μL

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 26

mg-1. These are the necessary features for developing lithium-sulfur batteries with a high energy density. [227] These novel cathode designs featuring a high amount of sulfur with lean electrolyte indicate the significant progress that is being made in developing cells under more realistic operating conditions that can enable high energy density. Such progress in optimizing the cell-assembly parameters will also benefit subsequent refinement in the battery chemistry and the electrochemical characteristics of the cell.

Electrolyte Formula: While most of the work in the area of lithium-sulfur cells has focused on developing the sulfur cathode, relatively little attention has been paid to considering and optimizing the electrolyte formula.[9,16,17,66,152-162] The electrolyte formula involves the selection of electrolyte solution, lithium salts, and auxiliary additives (Figure 4g-i). Carbonate- and ether-based electrolytes are two of the most commonly used systems, with lithium hexafluorophosphate (LiPF6) and lithium bis(trifluoromethanesulfonyl)imide (LITFSI), respectively, as the lithium salts. Approximately 95% of the reported electrolyte formulas are ether-based electrolyte, 90% of which are composed of a mixture of 1,3-dioxolane (DOL) and DME. Several different solution systems have been reported in early studies, such as poly(ethylene glycol) dimethyl ether (PEGDME), tetraethylene glycol dimethyl ether (TEGDME), and mixtures of DOL and TEGDME. However, after 2014, binary mixtures of DOL/DME became the most commonly used electrolyte solution because of their acceptable stability and compatibility toward polysulfides.[151-162] Thus, from 2014 till now, over 95% of lithium-sulfur studies use DOL/DME solvent for the electrolyte (Figure 4g and Table 1). The lithium salt is the source of lithium ions in the electrolyte. A suitable salt should feature high lithium-ion diffusion capability and create a stable interface environment for both electrodes. In

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 27

the commonly used DOL/DME solution, 87% of lithium-sulfur studies report using LITFSI as the lithium salt (Figure 4h). LITFSI is able to form a stable solid electrolyte interphase on the lithiummetal anode and offer high thermal stability and compatibility with ether solvents. Moreover, LITFSI has high dissociation ability and electrolyte conductivity.[228,229] Thus, LITFSI salt currently offers better cycling capability as compared to other salts.[16,229-232] Two additional salts, though less commonly used, are lithium trifluoromethanesulfonate (LITf) and lithium perchlorate (LiClO4), which are used in 8% and 1% of studies, respectively. However, LITf will corrode the aluminum current collector at 2.8 V and LiClO4 has safety concerns.[12,16,154,232] As a result, dissolving ~ 1.0 M LITFSI in DOL/DME solution with a volume ratio of 1:1 is the most frequently used electrolyte system in current lithium-sulfur research. However, though commonly used, this electrolyte formula might be an acceptable one for control experiments and might not be the best electrolyte due to the low Coulombic efficiency.[50,54,172,232-234] In order to improve the Coulombic efficiency to approach 99%, lithium-sulfur studies have also focused on the development of electrolyte additives as a co-salt. Lithium nitrate (LiNO3) additive is known to form a passivation layer on the lithium-metal anode surface, which reduces the redox shuttle of polysulfides and, therefore, helps to suppress the degradation of the anode.[16,203a,233] LiNO3 also helps oxidize lithium polysulfides to slightly soluble elemental sulfur near the end of cell charging.[234] The positive functions of LiNO3 for the two electrodes greatly improve the chargedischarge efficiency and electrochemical stability of lithium-sulfur cells. Therefore, since 2012, LiNO3 has become a necessary additive in lithium sulfur research. Over 90% of current lithium-sulfur studies report using LiNO3 in their electrolyte formula (Figure 4i).[16,50,233,234]

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 28

With an increasing focus on developing lithium-sulfur cells with a high amount of sulfur and reduced electrolyte volume, a higher concentration of LiNO3 of 0.2 – 1.0 M is added to the electrolyte (Figure 4i). Adding a high concentration of LiNO3 of over 0.1 M helps compensate for the redox capability of the sulfur cathode. More importantly, a high concentration of LiNO 3 in the electrolyte can assist in keeping the passivation layer intact on lithium anode for many cycles, which is essential for achieving long-life cells.

Lithium-metal Anode: The limitation posed on the electrochemical performance of lithium-sulfur cells by the irreversible loss of active material on the anode side is poorly understood.[169] Most lithium-sulfur studies have the investigated cells assembled in half-cell configuration with a thick lithium-metal foil as a counter electrode of the sulfur cathode. A large excess of lithium metal in the cell compensates for the irreversible losses of lithium during the repeated lithium-deposition process, but it results in an overestimated electrochemical performance and a significant reduction in the energy density of the assembled cells.[169,235] In order to accurately investigate the impact of coupling a high-capacity sulfur cathode with a lithium anode in a lithium-sulfur cell, the lithium-metal anode must not use excess lithium. A stoichiometric amount might offer new scientific insights, especially the understanding of the lithium-deposition process during lithium-sulfur cell cycling.[169] However, in consideration of the fact that lithium sulfides form on the surface of the reacted lithium-metal anode, 50% lithium excess might be necessary in conducting research to compensate for the consumption of lithium.[12,45-47,173] In addition, for the practical perspective, replacing the thick lithium foil with a thin anode can increase the energy density of the cell. A novel full-cell configuration fabricated with a Li2S cathode and a copper foil at the anode side of the cell enables the use of no excess lithium in a “lithium-

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 29

limited” lithium-sulfur cell. Such a cell configuration allows a reversible and stable plating/stripping process of lithium on the copper foil, representing a promising approach to a realistic and effective implementation of the lithium‐metal anode in lithium-sulfur cells with high energy density. [169]

2.3.3. Cell Testing and Evaluation After assembling a lithium-sulfur cell, the testing conditions can subsequently impact the electrochemical behavior of the device. Applying different cell-testing conditions may result in a very different cell performance because of the conversion reactions that happen during every discharging and charging process.[46,236-238] The cell testing conditions, including the operating voltage window and cycling rates, are two major factors that influence the performance evaluation characteristics of lithium-sulfur cells, including the charge-storage capacity, cycle stability, and cycle life.[12,46,66,90]

Operating Voltage Window: The operating voltage window affects the lithium-sulfur electrochemistry by its two ends: the cut-off voltages during the discharging and charging processes. The discharge cut-off voltage controls the level of reduction reactions from sulfur to lithium sulfides and ranges from 1.0 to 1.9 V in the literature (Figure 4j). Discharging the cell down to a low voltage of 1.5 V achieves a complete reduction to form sulfides and a full utilization of the active material. However, lithium sulfides formed at 1.5 V are hard to fully convert back, which implies a high capacity fade in the next cycle.[7,10,35,36,46] Moreover, the LiNO3 electrolyte additive, which can improve the redox reaction of sulfur and stabilize the reacted lithium anode, is irreversibly reduced at 1.6 V. The insoluble reduction products of LiNO3 on the surface of the cathode lower the reaction kinetics of the subsequent redox processes, paradoxically affecting the redox reversibility of the

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 30

sulfur cathode and leading to decreased cycling stability.[46,233,234] Therefore, a compromise demonstrated in current lithium-sulfur research is to stop discharging the cell to no lower than 1.7 V, trading the limited discharge capacity for the good reversibility of the cell. The tendency of keeping a relatively high discharge cut-off voltage to limit the reduction of LiNO3 increases with the increased use of LiNO3 in the electrolyte (Figure 4i and 4j). Over 70% of lithium-sulfur studies report the cell performance by discharging the testing cells to no lower than 1.7 V. Lithium-sulfur cells are usually charged to 2.5 – 3.0 V. Similar to what happens in the discharge cut-off voltage setting, the charge cut-off voltage also reconciles between a fully charged state at 3.0 V, achieving a complete oxidation of the sulfur species,[75,185,219] and a moderate state at 2.5 – 2.8 V, helping the cells maintain good cycle stability.[221,225,59] Charging a cell to 2.8 V is commonly used in lithium-sulfur studies. At 2.8 V, an almost full charge can be achieved, while avoiding the severe re-deposition and formation of clusters of solid end-charge products (Figure 4k).[12,59,218,222-226]

Cycling Rate: The cycling rate of the cell is another cell-testing parameter that can be used to balance the completion and reversibility of the redox reactions in lithium-sulfur cells. At a low cycling rate of less than C/10, elemental sulfur can be almost fully converted into lithium sulfide so that the cell obtains high discharge capacity values. However, a full conversion reaction also indicates that more lithium polysulfides might remain in the cycled cells and more insoluble lithium sulfides might cover the cathode, which limits the cycle life.[79-81,152,239] In contrast to a low cycling rate, a high cycling rate needs a high current density, which creates high electric force fields from the anode to the cathode during charging. By adjusting the operation rates, we can artificially reduce the

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 31

polysulfide migration from the cathode to the anode, resulting in improved cycling stability.[80,81,152,239] For example, many studies in lithium-sulfur research involve low-loading sulfur cathodes that utilize high cycling rates to contrive good cycle stability and long cycle life (Figure 4l and Table 1).[7,10,12,46,106] Therefore, comprehensive cyclability testing should consider the impact of both high and regular cycle rates on the sulfur utilization and cycle stability of the cell. Ideally, at a C/10 rate (or current densities lower than 168 mA g−1), the low current density can allow the study of polysulfide reactions and electrode degradation due to the sufficient reaction time and redox reactions.[12,106]

2.3.4. Impact Assessment Beyond developing lithium-sulfur cells with practical high energy density, current research is starting to consider the cost and environmental impact of lithium-sulfur technology. Cost and safety determine whether a potential technology is suitable for industrial production and commercial application. In view of fabrication feasibility and cost, it is preferable to use simple electrode configurations that are made with facile syntheses using safe and inexpensive raw materials. In addition, there is a necessity to transition lithium-sulfur technology from coin-type cells with small size electrodes (areal size: 1.0 cm2) to pouch-type cells with electrode sizes similar or close to that of practical battery electrodes (areal size: 40 cm2).[12,45-47,58,59,210] Many reported high-performance sulfur cathodes have a special and unique configuration or nanostructure. Complex fabrication methods are commonly used in the literature along with the need for toxic raw materials and/or by-products. Such designs are not suitable for large-scale production or use and are in contradiction with the original intent of developing lithium-sulfur

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 32

technology with inexpensive electrode materials and good environmental compatibility. However, based on the knowledge established with the sophisticated sulfur cathodes, the research community has utilized similar concepts to develop simpler electrodes that are more compatible with practical applications. The simple cathode designs use low-cost, commercially available materials as well as easy preparation processes while still achieving high electrochemical performance.[4,7,10-12,46] As a section summary, for a battery to be practically viable, there are more considerations than just the energy density, such as safety, cost, and cycle-life. Thus, when considering the future feasibility of lithium-sulfur batteries, we should combine current advances in electrochemical performance with the cell-assembly and cell-testing/evaluation conditions. It is very possible that lithium-sulfur batteries can still be practical even if the energy density is not greater than lithium-ion batteries. As long as the cells can be made safer (e.g, polysulfides can react with lithium dendrites to mitigate short circuit issues) in a cost-effective manner (e.g., lower cost of the sulfur electrode), there is a good angle for its commercial adoption.

3. Metal-Sulfur Cells Despite advances made in lithium-sulfur batteries, there are still intrinsic and extrinsic challenges that must be overcome to make this technology commercially viable. As we learned from the discussion of progress made in lithium-sulfur research, the lithium-metal anode has not yet obtained a significant breakthrough, particularly compared to what has been achieved by intensive cathode studies (Figure 4 and Table 1). However, it would be reasonable to explore new electrochemical conversion battery systems that can couple with the high-capacity sulfur cathode. Moreover, the use of lithium-metal anodes faces an intrinsic challenge in that lithium metal has a

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 33

strong activity and forms dendrites during cell operation, which leads to a low Coulombic efficiency, poor cycle stability, and safety concerns.[108,173,240] Besides the battery chemistry, the rapid expansion of lithium-ion technology in various applications has led to the increasing price of lithium. The cost of raw materials needed for lithiumion batteries, such as Li2CO3, reached over $ 12,000 per ton this year (August, 2018), which is twice as high as it was in 2015 and approximately 80 times higher than sulfur, which costs just ~ $ 150 per ton.[241-244] Apart from the increasing price of lithium itself, unlike sulfur, lithium is unevenly sourced, being distributed in hard rock deposits mostly in Australia and in liquid brines along the border of Chile and Argentina. Moreover, the relative abundance of lithium in the crust of Earth is low (Figure 5).[18-22,243] The anticipation of the increasing price and limited availability of lithium has become a strong driving force in the investigation of other alkali and alkaline earth metals (e.g., sodium,[1821,31,244,245]

potassium,[22-24,31,244,246] magnesium,[18,25-28,31,244,247] and calcium[31,244,248]) as well as

aluminum metal[18,29-31,244,249,250] as a metallic anode (Figure 5a-d). These elements are either available in the ocean or much richer in the Earth’s crust compared to lithium (Figure 5e). The relatively even distribution and high abundance make them easy to obtain and exploit.[31,244-250] In addition to addressing the cost and limitation issues of lithium-metal anodes, some of these other metal anodes also have a high reduction potential that is close to that of lithium-metal anode and can contribute acceptably high charge-storage capacities (Figure 5f). They enable the assembled cells to deliver sufficient voltage and capacity, which should produce satisfactory energy densities for practical applications (Figure 5g,h).[18-22,243-250] As a result, the coupling of these metallic anodes with sulfur cathodes should behave similar to their lithium counterpart, resulting in a number of new

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 34

conversion reactions with high energy density. Moreover, adopting the mature sulfur cathode technology might shorten the research timeline for the development of these low-cost metals for commercial batteries. In the following section, we discuss the current progress made in other metalsulfur batteries based on lithium-sulfur technology.

3.1. Sodium-Sulfur Cells High-temperature sodium-sulfur batteries that operate at 300 – 350 °C are an established commercial battery system used for stationary energy-storage applications.[251] They possess molten electrodes and a solid beta-alumina electrolyte to deliver reasonable power and energy densities, high electrochemical efficiency, and long cycle life.[18,19,251a-251c] At high temperatures, the melted sodium and sulfur are highly corrosive so that alloyed steels are used in cell assembly. Unfortunately, the high operating temperature and cost block the wide application of high-temperature sodiumsulfur batteries.[251d,251e] These limitations have led the research community to explore roomtemperature sodium-sulfur batteries that adopt the advantages of the high-temperature sodiumsulfur system at low-temperatures and safer operating conditions.[18-20,251f] Moreover, applying highperformance sulfur cathodes from lithium-sulfur batteries to sodium-sulfur cells has demonstrated great improvements in rapidly developing the room-temperature sodium-sulfur systems although they are at their infancy.[18-21,252] Room-temperature sodium-sulfur batteries offer a high theoretical charge-storage capacity and energy density of, respectively, 1,672 mA h g-1 and 1,230 W h kg-1 with an assumption of full discharge reactions from sulfur to sodium sulfide (Na2S) (Figure 5).[20-22,244] However, sodium-sulfur batteries also inherit similar electrochemical challenges as lithium-sulfur technology, such as low

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 35

electrochemical utilization, reversibility, and efficiency.[19,252,253] Additional challenges derive from the complicated reaction mechanism of sulfur and sodium, which is dependent on the electrolyte system. Progress in sodium-sulfur battery research has been slowed by the low reactivity of solid sulfur and sodium ions in the liquid electrolyte that is currently used. The resulting incomplete reduction not only causes low active-material utilization but also the formation of sodium polysulfide with the chemical formula of Na2Sx (x ≧ 2). These drawbacks amplify the poor electrochemical utilization and stability of sodium-sulfur cells, resulting in low capacity, poor cycle life, and slow cyclability.[19,20] Moreover, sodium-sulfur studies would need to address the extrinsic problems of the system by optimizing the cell-assembly parameters, including a high sulfur loading, a sufficient sulfur content, and a low amount of electrolyte in the cell.[244,245,252,253]

3.1.1. Sodium-sulfur Electrochemistry The sodium-sulfur battery configuration is similar to that of the lithium-sulfur design. In this case, the sodium metal that replaces the lithium functions as the anode while a porous cathode substrate functions as a nanocomposite to host the sulfur. In between these two electrodes, a separator is added to prevent short circuits. Normally, organic solvent containing sodium salt is used as the electrolyte to transport the sodium ions.[252,253] In 2006, Park et al. proposed the concept of a sodium-sulfur cell that can operate normally at room temperature using a nanocomposite sulfur cathode, a sodium-metal anode, and a PVDF gel polymer electrolyte with sodium trifluoromethanesulfonate (NaCF3SO3).[252] Theoretically, the sodium-sulfur cell is assembled at the charged state. During discharge, the sodium metal is oxidized and generates sodium ions and electrons, which travel from the metal

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 36

anode to the sulfur cathode through the electrolyte in the cell and the external circuit, respectively. The sulfur cathode that accepts sodium ions and electrons is reduced into sodium polysulfides (Na2Sx, x = 4 – 8) with the end-discharge producing sodium sulfide (Na2S).[19,20,244] During charge, the reversible conversion reaction starts from the oxidation of sodium sulfides and proceeds to the formation of sodium polysulfides and sulfur. As a result, the overall reversible redox reactions are S + 2Na = Na2S (e.g., S8 + 16Na = 8Na2S with element S8) with 2Na = 2Na+ + 2e- (e.g., 16Na = 16Na+ + 16e) at the anode side and S + 2e- = S2- (e.g., S8 + 16e- = 8 S2- with element S8) at the cathode side. Each sulfur atom can operate with a pair of electron and sodium ions, which results in a high chargestorage capacity and energy density of the system.[19-21,252] The redox reactions between the two solid-state end-redox products, sulfur at the fully charged state and sodium sulfides at the fully discharged state, involve the generation of a group of liquid-state sodium polysulfides. The conversion reactions of these materials are quite similar to the lithium-sulfur counterpart. The compositional and structural conversions of sulfur, sodium polysulfides, and sodium sulfides involve four continuous stages. During the discharge process, sulfur is reduced to form long-chain sodium polysulfide (Na2S8) at the upper discharge plateau (~ 2.20 V), which is a phase transition from solid-state to liquid-state active material.[19,20,252] From 2.20 to 1.65 V, mixtures of sodium polysulfides form and theoretically convert from Na2S8 to Na2S4. The conversion between different sodium polysulfides is a liquid-to-liquid transition, which allows the polysulfides to easily dissolve into the electrolyte. Thus, this step is usually reflected as a discharge shoulder in the discharge curve. At ~ 1.65 V, a lower discharge plateau forms, which corresponds to the conversion from the liquid-state Na2S4 to solid-state mixtures of Na2S3/Na2S2. After discharging

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 37

the cell to 1.2 V or lower voltage, another shoulder appears at 1.65 – 1.20 V. This slow solid-solid transition involves the reduction from Na2S2 to the end-discharge product Na2S.[18-20,252,254]

3.1.2. Sodium-Sulfur Electrochemical Performance Not only do sodium-sulfur cells adopt the desirable advantages of lithium-sulfur batteries, both being low-cost energy-storage devices with high energy densities, they also experience similar disruptive challenges. Both lithium and sodium metal anodes are coupled to a sulfur cathode. The generated sodium polysulfides have higher solubility in the electrolyte as compared to the lithium polysulfides. This high solubility increases the mobility and activity of the dissolved sodium polysulfides, which results in a strong shuttle effect that impacts the electrochemical reversibility and efficiency of the sodium-sulfur system.[254-259] The redox reaction between sulfur and sodium sulfides also causes a huge volume change. Fully sodiated sulfur can attain a volume expansion of up to ~ 170%, which is more than two-times greater than the lithium-sulfur battery cathode.[251b,251c,260,261] As a reference, a 80% volume change in a lithium-sulfur battery cathode is strong enough to pulverize the cathode integrity.[12,84,166-168] In the anode region, similar dendrite formation and surface corrosion problems befall the sodium-metal anode.[18,19,261-264] First, the large size change between the sodium atom and ion causes unstable sodium deposition, resulting in sodium dendrites.[263] Moreover, sodium has a stronger activity compared to lithium. Severe reaction with the electrolyte at the electrode/electrolyte interface and an unstable SEI layer, therefore, occur on the sodium-metal anode, which may create even more electrochemical instability and inefficiency than lithium-metal anode.[20,262,263] One more unique drawback of sodium-sulfur cells is the larger cationic size. Sodium ions have an ionic radius of

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 38

0.102 nm, while the radius of lithium ion is only 0.076 nm (Figure 5c). Thus, sodium ions have lower mobility than lithium ions in a cell, which aggravates the sluggish redox reaction with sulfur and enlarges the polarization of the cells. The integration of low sodium-ion diffusion in the cell and high sodium-polysulfide dissolution in the electrolyte causes a fast capacity fade during cell cycling and resting. The severe sodium instability results in a low Coulombic efficiency and incites strong safety concerns.[19-21,251c] Studies addressing these intrinsic material challenges have mainly focused on converting what has been previously reported in lithium-sulfur research to the sodium-sulfur system, with most significant achievements occurring in the sulfur cathode region of the cell (Table 2).[18,19,252,266-270] The fabrication of sulfur-based nanocomposite cathodes is the mainstream research direction, involving the integration of sulfur with conductive agents and infusing sulfur into a porous carbon host.[251c,253,268,269] Apart from the design of the nanomaterial, the engineering design of the cell configuration has also been demonstrated to enhance the electrochemical performance of sodiumsulfur cells. Promising methods include the modification of the current collector[245,259,270-272] and functional separators.[245,272,273] A relatively limited number of studies have focused on modifications to the electrolyte composition and formula due to the complicated sodium/sulfur redox reactions.[251c,252,261,265,274-276] Several contributions have demonstrated high sulfur utilization of over 70% (a charge-storage capacity of over 1,170 mA h g-1 sulfur) in different advanced sodium-sulfur cells using hollow carbon spheres[264,268] and porous carbon nanofiber substrates (Figure 6a).[277] In recent years, long cycle lives of over 1,000 cycles at fast cycling rates have been demonstrated in sodium-sulfur cells fabricated using a carbon nanotube porous current collector at 0.6C rate,[270] with a composite cathode made of microporous carbon spheres at 1.0C rate to attain 1,500 cycles,[253a]

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 39

and with a sulfur-based nanocomposite having a carbon host mixed with BaTiO3 and TiO2 at 1.2C rate to achieve 3,000 cycles.[259] In addition, the integration of a carbon-nanofiber cathode substrate with a carbon-nanofiber-coated Nafion membrane offers the designed cells with a high capacity retention of over 91% after 100 cycles (Figure 6b,c).[245a] These achievements imply that utilizing cathode designs brought about by lithium-sulfur research does benefit progress in sodium-sulfur cell performance, and more importantly, may decrease the research time of the sodium-sulfur system in its development toward long cycle life.

3.1.3. Performance Testing and Evaluation Research progress in sodium-sulfur cells is similar to what has been done in the lithiumsulfur system and has mostly focused on improving the sodium-sulfur cell performance from the perspective of the sulfur cathode. Consequently, it is instructive to explore the cell-assembly parameters, the electrolyte systems, and the performance testing and evaluation of current sodiumsulfur studies (Figure 6d-f).

Cell-Assembly Parameters: Sodium-sulfur cells have a relatively low operating voltage compared to lithium-sulfur cells. A greater amount of sulfur in the cathode is necessary to attain a sufficiently high effective capacity and energy density in order to be competitive with lithium-sulfur or commercial lithium-ion cells. Additionally, at least 60 wt.% sulfur in the cathode is also a practical condition for understanding the sluggish electrochemical redox reactions in the sodium-sulfur system. However, less than 15% of sodium-sulfur battery studies from 2006 to 2018 have reported a sufficient sulfur content of over 60 wt.% in the cathode (Figure 6d). The average sulfur content reported in the

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 40
