cathode is still low, typically less than 50 wt.%, without the consideration of the mass of the current collector. An even greater challenge compared to the ubiquitous problems of low sulfur content is the low sulfur loading applied in the testing of sodium-sulfur cells. Although no current study has demonstrated the cell electrochemistry and/or performance based on a cathode with sufficient sulfur loading of 5 mg cm-2, the trend in sulfur loading has at least begun to increase since 2016 (Figure 6e). In 2018, the trend of integrating the optimization of the cathode configuration and electrolyte formula has suggested a promising route toward the improvement of sulfur loading in sodium-sulfur battery electrochemistry, with a low sulfur loading of 0.35 mg cm-2 increasing to a high sulfur loading of 4.64 mg cm-2 with good cycle stability. The application of multiporous carbon fibers as the cathode substrate and 10 mM InI3 as an electrolyte additive has also improved both the electrochemical and chemical stabilities of the sulfur cathode and sodium anode, respectively.[277] The high-sulfur-loading measurement reported in this work offers further evidence suggesting that the amount of sulfur plays the most significant role in the electrochemical reactions of sodium-sulfur cells.[277] With low sulfur loadings of 0.35 – 1.57 mg cm-2, cells show similar electrochemical characteristics with a high discharge capacity of over 1,000 mA h g-1 for 50 cycles. As the sulfur loading increases to over 4.0 mg cm-2, the discharge capacity of the cell drops to lower than 1,000 mA h g-1 after 1 cycle and decreases to less than 400 mA h g-1 after 50 cycles.[277] This negative feature suggests that the sluggish reaction kinetics of high-loading sulfur cathodes become even worse when they are equipped in sodium-sulfur cells due to the slow diffusion of the sodium ions and the poor reaction between sodium and sulfur.[18-20,46,277] The stagnant reaction kinetics and high polarization of sodium-sulfur cells cause studies to use high electrolyte/sulfur ratios of over 20 μL

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 41

mg-1 or else this parameter is not reported at all (Figure 6f). However, a high electrolyte/sulfur ratio in cell is in opposite directions toward understanding the sodium-sulfur battery chemistry and developing them as high-energy-density batteries.[12,46,66,76-79]

Electrolyte Formula: In addition to analyzing the cell-assembly parameters used in sodium-sulfur cells, we also explored the effect of the electrolyte formula (Figure 6g,h). As a newly developed battery system, various carbonate- and ether-based electrolytes have been used as the electrolyte in sodium-sulfur cells for optimizing the electrode/electrolyte interfacial stability, facilitating sodiumion diffusion, and reducing the fast dissolution and diffusion of polysulfides.[16,54,255-258,278] The same electrolyte formula is borrowed from lithium-sulfur battery electrolyte and modified for use in the sodium-sulfur system. Over 40% of sodium-sulfur battery studies report using TEGDME, making it the most commonly used ether-based electrolyte solution (Figure 6g), and typically incorporating either 0.5 – 1.0 M sodium trifluoromethanesulfonate (NaOTf) or 1.5 M sodium perchlorate (NaClO4) as the sodium salt (Figure 6g,h).[253a,255,258,261,261,272,273,279-281] On the other hand, almost half of the carbonate-based electrolyte used in sodium-sulfur batteries feature a mixed solution of ethylene carbonate/propylene carbonate (EC/PC) at a 1:1 volume/volume ratio, which is also the most commonly used carbonate-based electrolyte. Due to its binary solution characteristics, more and more studies tend to use EC/PC as the electrolyte solution.[251c,260,270,271,274,282-284] In both carbonateand ether-based solution systems, NaClO4 is added at a concentration of 1.0 – 1.5 M as the salt (Figure 6h).[267-269,271,280,282-284] The addition of 5 wt.% fluoroethylene carbonate (FEC) in the electrolyte also helps to generate a FEC-based interfacial layer on the reacted sodium anode, which

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 42

reduces the anode corrosion during cycling.[268,274,285,286] As a result, most studies reported in 2018 used 1.0 M NaClO4 in EC/PC (1:1 v/v) with additional 5 wt.% FEC.[282-286] FEC has also recently been used with PC to form PC/FEC (1:1 v/v) electrolyte solution.[19,277,287] FEC in the electrolyte solution works with 2.0 M NaClO4 salt to reduce the fast dissolution of the polysulfides and also form a stable SEI layer on the anode surface. Additionally, the use of InI3 as a redox catalyst in the electrolyte has been reported to form a passivating indium layer on the cycled anode, which further improves the electrode stability.[18,19,277]

Cell-Testing Conditions: The cell-assembly parameters and electrolyte formula will influence the electrochemical behavior of the sodium-sulfur system. This implies that cell-testing conditions will also determine the resulting cell performance (Figure 6i,j). Although lithium-/sodium-sulfur cells both have multi-step redox reactions, the large size and poor mobility of sodium ions cause the electrochemical reactions of sodium-sulfur cells to be complex. The sluggish reaction kinetics causes the high polarization in sodium-sulfur cells so that the operating voltage window shifts toward lower discharge (0.5 – 1.4 V) and higher charge cut-off voltages (2.1 – 3.4 V). Current studies tend to use relatively low discharge cut-off voltages of 0.5 V (reported by 15% and 33% studies, respectively, till and in 2018) and 0.8 V (reported by 30% and 33% studies, respectively, till and in 2018). A deep discharge stage is, therefore, necessary to achieve higher electrochemical utilization and a more complete reaction as the trend in sodium-sulfur battery cathodes has been to feature increasing sulfur loadings (Figure 6i). Coupling with these two discharge voltages are the high charge cut-off voltages of up to 2.8 and 3.0 V (Figure 6j). Therefore,

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 43

the commonly used operating voltage windows include 1.0 – 3.0 V and 0.8 – 2.8 V (Figure 6i,j and Table 2). Besides borrowing sulfur cathodes from lithium-sulfur research and developing new electrolyte composition for sodium-sulfur cells, the sodium-metal anode is a new and necessary area to explore in future research, particularly in terms of its stability and redox reversibility. Suitable celldesign parameters and testing conditions should also be applied in sodium-sulfur research to evaluate the feasibility of the proposed designs. This is because the sodium-sulfur system operates via reversible conversion reactions so that its electrochemical characteristics are sensitive toward the cell-design parameters and testing conditions.

3.2. Potassium-Sulfur Cells Potassium metal is currently being developed as a new member of the metal-sulfur battery family, adopting the mature technology from lithium-sulfur research[12,45-47,58-66] and the rapid progress made in sodium-sulfur cells (Figure 5).[18-21] The high abundance of potassium in Earth’s crust and in the ocean reduces production and transport costs of the raw materials (Figure 5e).[243,288290]

Potassium metal has a lower standard reduction potential (-2.93 v.s. SHE) than other metallic

elements beyond lithium. Thus, applying potassium as the anode material in a potassium-sulfur cell should offer high specific capacity and energy density (Figure 5f-h).[22,288] Therefore, although in the infancy stage, the investigation and development of potassium-sulfur batteries is in high demand in order to understand the technology’s feasibility.[22,288-290]

3.2.1. Potassium-sulfur Electrochemistry

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 44

Zhao et al. proposed the potassium-sulfur battery electrochemistry in 2014.[22,31,288] This pioneering work reported a potassium-metal thin foil as the anode and a polyaniline-coated sulfur/porous carbon (CMK-3) nanocomposite as the cathode. Initiated from a composite sulfur cathode, the first potassium-sulfur cell operates through conversion reaction between sulfur and dipotassium trisulfide (K2S3) at room temperature.[288] After that, limited follow-up work was reported until 2017 and 2018 due to the severe intrinsic challenges brought about by the use of the potassium-metal anode and the sulfur cathode, such as high cathode resistance, fast active-material loss, and electrochemical instability (Table 2).[18,22,31] In order to address these challenges, current research has mainly focused on confining sulfur within the porous carbon host[288,289,291] and bonding sulfur as sulfur-polyacrylonitrile compounds.[243,290,292] There two methods have been deeply studied in lithium-sulfur research, demonstrating the ability to provide facile transport of electrons and ions in the cathode and a strong ability to chemically trap sulfur species for improved stability and reactivity.[7,10,12,22] The proposed conversion reactions in potassium-sulfur cells fabricated with sulfur/carbon composite cathodes involve the phase transition from sulfur to K2S3 when the cathode accepts potassium ions through the electrolyte and electrons through the external electrical circuit. In between sulfur and K2S3, potassium polysulfides (K2Sx, x = 4 – 6) are formed.[288,291] K2S2 and K2S are difficult to form as the end-discharge products due to the incomplete reduction for K2S3 to reduce its sulfur chains. As a result, the overall reversible redox reaction is 3S + 2K = K2S3, with the reversible discharge and charge reactions being 3S + 2K+ + 2e- = K2S3 and K2S3 - 2e- = 3S + 2K+.[22,288] In 2018, the use of sulfurized polyacrylonitrile as the starting active material demonstrated the formation of K 2S2

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 45

and K2S when the discharge cut-off voltage was set at a low 0.1 V for the sulfurized-polyacrylonitrile cathode.[243,290] The redox reactions of the intermediate potassium sulfides involve the phase transformations from sulfur to K2Sx (with x = 3 – 6).[31,291] Specifically, as the cell is discharged to ~ 2.1 V, high-order potassium polysulfides (K2Sx, x = 5 – 6) are generated that can readily dissolve in commonly used ether-based electrolytes. Insoluble potassium sulfides (K2Sx, x = 3 – 4) form during the successive discharge and convert to low-order K2S3 at ~ 1.8 V.[289,291] In the reversible charging process, two redox reactions occur at ~ 2.05 and 2.30 V.[288,289]

3.2.2. Potassium-sulfur Electrochemical Performance As the metallic anode is replaced from lithium and sodium to potassium, the existing slow reaction problems brought about by the insulating sulfur cathode become much more severe due to the new material challenges brought about by potassium. Potassium ions have a large radius of 0.138 nm, which is almost two times larger than lithium ions that already suffer from a sluggish reaction with the sulfur cathode (Figure 5c). More importantly, the large ionic size of potassium ions results in high polarization and a large cathode volume change.[288,289,291] The sluggish reaction capability and high polarization of potassium-sulfur system are usually reflected in the incomplete redox reaction. The reversible end-discharge product commonly reported of the potassium-sulfur cell is K2S3; however, more complete reduction products, Li2S and Na2S2, are reported in the lithiumand sodium-sulfur cells.[18,20-22,288] The huge volume change in the cathode needs potassium-sulfur

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 46

studies to have more efforts on electrode engineering.[289] During the charging process, in order to attain a complete conversion reaction, most potassium-sulfur studies apply a high charge cutoff voltage of 2.4 V and 3.0 V.[288-292] Recently, it has been shown that charging the cells back to K2S5 at 1.85 V is one possibility that can improve the electrochemical reversibility due to the high activity of the formed potassium polysulfide catholyte.[289] Although increasing studies have focused on the potassium-sulfur system in 2018, the electrochemical performance is not as strong as that of lithium-/sodium-sulfur cells (Figure 7 and Table 2). Despite adopting the well-developed sulfur cathodes, the low-diffusion capability of potassium ions and their poor reactivity with sulfur results in a low average sulfur utilization of 36% (Figure 7a). Moreover, the strong activity of potassium in the electrochemical cell further damages the cyclability and stability. Most potassium-sulfur studies require a low cycling rate of less than 0.1C rate to make the cell cycle normally. However, at a low cycling rate, the potassium-sulfur cells reported still encounter challenges in achieving a high electrochemical utilization and long cycle stability (Figure 7b,c). In 2018, a potassium-sulfur cell fabricated with a coated separator having single wall carbon nanotubes as the coating material displayed a high charge-storage capacity of 1,140 mA h g-1[246] and a sulfurized polyacrylonitrile cathode with polyacrylic acid binder attained a high reversible capacity of 1050 mA h g-1 after 100 cycles.[243] These high-performance cell designs are promising for the development of potassium-sulfur cells with enhanced electrochemical characteristics.[22,31,243,246]

3.2.3. Performance Testing and Evaluation

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 47

Potassium-sulfur cells also feature challenges associated with the cell-design parameters used to investigate the cell electrochemistry, performance, and feasibility. Although potassiumsulfur studies have applied the advanced sulfur cathodes that worked well in lithium-sulfur cells, state-of-the-art potassium-sulfur cells once again need the following conditions to achieve the previously described performance characteristics: low sulfur content (average ~ 37 wt.%, Figure 7d) and a low sulfur loading (average ~ 0.84 mg cm-2, Figure 7e) with high electrolyte/sulfur ratios of over 48 μL mg-1. The use of sulfur/carbon nanocomposites with such a low amount of sulfur in the cathode raises concerns about our understanding of the true reaction capability as well as the possibly low energy density of the cell. On the other hand, the sulfur-polyacrylonitrile compounds usually have a very low sulfur content (< 50 wt.%), which results in a low amount of sulfur in the cathode and low effective capacity.[243,290,292] Therefore, it is likely that the performance of the potassium-sulfur system will be exceedingly poor when the time comes to apply more practical conditions for assembling the cell.[12,22,46,66] Current studies of potassium-sulfur cells have focused on the development of nanocomposite sulfur/carbon and sulfurized-polyacrylonitrile cathodes. Thus, the operation conditions have mainly been optimized to match these kinds of sulfur cathodes. An operating voltage window of 1.2 – 2.4 V is commonly used for the potassium anode–sulfur/carbon composite cathode system.[288,289,291] The upper cut-off potential is kept below 2.4 V because the overactivity between the metallic potassium and K2Sx above this value leads to detrimental shuttling reactions. However, optimization of the operating voltage window has not yet been performed because a suitable electrolyte formula remains unknown and the underlying reaction mechanisms have been obtained with cells fabricated with low sulfur loading and content.[22,288,289] On the other hand, a

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 48

voltage window between 0.1 and 3.0 V has been reported for cells featuring sulfurizedpolyacrylonitrile cathodes. However, because of the low amount of sulfur, the electrochemical performance of the sulfurized-polyacrylonitrile cathode is still far below that required for practical applications of potassium-sulfur batteries.[22,66,243,292] To summarize, as a result of using the insulating sulfur cathode, potassium-sulfur research has already encountered many of the same problems as lithium- and sodium-sulfur cells, such as the low reactivity of the metal anodes with the insulating sulfur, polysulfide diffusion, and the electrode instability. Moreover, the potassium-sulfur cell has a slower charge-transfer capability, more sluggish reaction kinetics, and higher volume changes as compared to lithium- and sodium-sulfur cells, which will create additional difficulties. Although new research directions appear from these materials challenges, the intensive application of idealized conditions in collecting data ultimately amplifies these difficulties and delays the development of the battery electrochemistry.[288,289,291]

3.3. Magnesium-sulfur Cells The research progress in lithium-sulfur technology and the growth of metal-sulfur cells with other alkali-metal candidates have inspired the application of alkaline earth metals. For example, magnesium metal has a high enough operating voltage and charge-storage capacity to generate a comparable gravimetric energy density with lithium-sulfur batteries. Moreover, the high valence (2+) and high density of magnesium metals result in promising volumetric energy densities for the magnesium-sulfur cells (Figure 5).[18,25] Magnesium metal generates two electrons during cell discharging, which doubles the charge-storage capacity. Moreover, considering its relatively high density (1.74 g cm-3), magnesium

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 49

offers a high volumetric specific capacity of 3,832 mA h cm-3, which is two times higher than that provided by lithium (2,062 mA h cm-3) (Figure 5a-d).[293,294] Besides the high capacity output, the magnesium metal could be developed as a safe metal anode because it does not form dendrites during repetitive striping and plating.[25,293,294] Ideally, a safe magnesium-sulfur cell would be able to offer a high theoretical gravimetric and volumetric energy densities of, respectively, 1,330 Wh kg-1 and 2,500 W h L-1.[25,26a] Besides the attractive performance, magnesium and sulfur are abundant elements in the Earth’s crust, which makes magnesium-sulfur cells a safe, low-cost and sustainable energy-storage system (Figure 5e-h).[25,26a,294] The current research breakthroughs have aimed to design compatible electrolytes for the high-valent magnesium-metal anodes to effectively and reversibly transport the magnesium ions in magnesium-sulfur cells. The electrolyte needs to avoid the formation of a blocking layer that shields the magnesium-metal anode. Moreover, the magnesium-sulfur battery electrolyte needs a wide electrochemical window.[25-28] Otherwise, the current magnesium-sulfur studies have reported that the plating and stripping of magnesium impacts the electrochemical reversibility and stability of the cells.[25,27] Besides the new challenges brought about by the magnesium-metal anode, polysulfides also form in magnesium-sulfur cells during intermediate charging and discharging.[10,12,25] As a result, the known issues associated with polysulfides (e.g., the shuttling effect, overcharge, and activematerial loss) also occur as these problems have not yet been resolved in any alkali (earth)-metal sulfur system.

3.3.1. Magnesium-sulfur Electrochemistry

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 50

In 2011, Kim et al. made a rechargeable magnesium-sulfur cell using hexamethyldisilazide magnesium chloride (HMDSMgCl) and AlCl3 as the electrolyte.[295] The non-nucleophilic electrolyte has HMDSMgCl, a non-nucleophilic base that enables the reversible magnesium deposition, and AlCl3 as the Lewis acid.[295,296] Magnesium foil was used as the anode, while a mixture of sulfur, carbon, and binder was applied onto a porous carbon substrate for the cathode.[295] Although the cell showed a fast capacity fade from 1,200 to 395 mA h g-1 in two cycles, this system demonstrated the possibility of making rechargeable magnesium-sulfur cells and the importance of investigating a suitable magnesium-sulfur battery electrolyte.[25-28] Magnesium-sulfur cells are also assembled at the charged state and can be directly discharged to form MgS and generate electricity. The high theoretical charge-storage capacity results from a two-electron electrochemical conversion, with the anode reaction as Mg = Mg2+ + 2eand the cathode reaction as S + 2e- = S2-, resulting in a reversible reaction of S + Mg = MgS.[18] Current research has revealed the main electrochemical reactions of sulfur with magnesium ions, which involves the conversion from sulfur to magnesium polysulfides (MgSx, x > 2) and from polysulfides to the end-discharge products (mixtures of MgS2 and MgS) during discharging.[25,26,297,298] Another reaction mechanism is reported with the use of LiTFSI additive as a Li+-ion mediator, which affects the activation of the MgSx species, improving the reaction reversibility.[18,26a,299] The discharge and charge mechanisms include the reduction reaction from sulfur to MgxLiyS3-8 and then MgxLiyS3 at ~ 1.75 V and the formation of MgS2/MgS and Li2S2/Li2S mixtures at ~ 0.5 V. The oxidation reaction shows a Li+-supported reactivation of MgS2/MgS mixtures back to MgxLiyS3 and MgxLiyS8 at 2.7 – 2.8 V.[298,299] During the initial discharge, a high initial polarization appears due to the formation of a

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 51

passivation film on the magnesium-metal anode. After the initial activation, a discharge plateau shows at ~ 1.0 – 0.8 V.[298,299]

3.3.2. Magnesium-sulfur Electrochemical Performance For practical applications, we next look into the cell performance and cell-assembly parameters currently reported in the magnesium-sulfur literature (Figure 8 and Table 2). Magnesium-sulfur cells face difficulty in attaining an acceptable electrochemical utilization of sulfur of at least 70%. The average sulfur utilization reported in magnesium-sulfur studies is just 46%, with the highest utilization rate of 74% achieved with the use of organic magnesium borate-based electrolyte (Figure 8a).[300] Maintaining stable and reversible cyclability of 100 cycles has also been a challenge. The cyclability of magnesium-sulfur cells is only ~ 55 cycles on average (Figure 8b). Moreover, the rate capability of magnesium-sulfur reported is low. A high fraction of studies uses a very low rate of C/100 to report the electrochemical reversibility and efficiency of the electrolyte and cathode (Figure 8c).[18,294,301-303] In addition, due to the high overpotential and overcharge behavior, magnesium-sulfur cells have poor Coulombic efficiency. However, the Coulombic efficiency was only first reported after 2017 for understanding the electrochemical reversibility of the cells and their charging and discharging reactions (Table 2).[297,298,300,304-306] The poor cyclability results from two major problems. First, the use of the insulating sulfur cathode causes the sluggish reaction kinetics between sulfur and MgS2/MgS mixtures in the magnesium-sulfur cells.[25-27,294,301-303] Second, the use of high-valent magnesium metal requires a new, stable electrolyte that can accommodate reversible magnesium stripping/plating and fast magnesium-ion transfer, as well as ensure the stability of the electrode. Although various

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 52

magnesium-ion electrolytes are reported, challenges remain, such as the low stripping/plating efficiency of magnesium, corrosion of the cell components, and the formation of unstable passivation layers on the magnesium-metal anode.[18,300,306,307] The performance enhancement of magnesium-sulfur cells has been demonstrated with the use of porous carbon nanofibers as the cathode substrate for improving the reaction capability. Additionally, a carbon-nanofiber-coated separator applied in the cell limits the polysulfide loss and effectively facilitates the electrochemical utilization with a high peak discharge capacity approaching 1,200 mAh g-1.[247a] In 2018, enhanced cycling performance was reported in a magnesium-sulfur cell fabricated with a magnesium metal as the anode, a Whatman glass fiber as the separator, and N/Co-doped porous carbon for the prepared composite sulfur cathode.[298] The cells utilized the (HMDS)2Mg-AlCl3-LiTFSI electrolyte[298,299] and attained a relatively high cycling rate of 0.1C for 250 cycles and an even higher cycling rate of up to 5C rate for 10 cycles in the rate-performance testing.[298]

3.3.3. Performance Testing and Evaluation Magnesium-sulfur cells have a high overpotential due to the slow diffusion and conversion of the multivalent species. Unfortunately, magnesium-sulfur studies have not yet found an electrolyte featuring a combination of good ionic conductivity, electrolyte viscosity, and polysulfide stability.[300,306] These drawbacks aggravate the sluggish reaction kinetics in the sulfur cathode.[18,76-79] As a result, magnesium-sulfur studies have tended to use idealized conditions. The conditions involve poor cell-assembly parameters, including a low amount of sulfur and a high/unknown

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 53

amount of electrolyte, which lead to poor practical feasibility compared to alkali-metal-sulfur cells (Figure 8 and Table 2). Specifically, only half of the magnesium-sulfur research articles report the cell-assembly parameters used. However, the reported sulfur content and loading were only, respectively, 42 wt.% and 1.1 mg cm-2 on average (Figure 8d,e). Only two electrolyte/sulfur ratios have been reported, which were up to 80 μL mg-1. Using these idealized conditions suggests that the reported reversibility of current magnesium-sulfur cells has been over-estimated. In addition, magnesiumsulfur cells mainly operate with 0.5 – 0.6 V as the cut-off discharge voltage coupled with 2.5 – 2.6 V as the cut-off charge voltage (Table 2).[298,303,304,306] This low operating voltage also suggests that magnesium-sulfur cells do need to use a sufficiently high amount of active material and low electrolyte volume in order to attain high effective capacities. Clearly, magnesium-sulfur cells are still in their infancy. Current research progress has demonstrated the improvement in the electrochemical reversibility, although there is a continuing search for a suitable electrolyte formulation.[295,297-299,303-306] Magnesium-sulfur studies have reported that different magnesium-ion electrolytes typically exhibit different voltage profiles.[27,28,298,299] This suggests that more studies should be focused on the electrolyte for further improvement of the magnesium-sulfur system. With a suitable electrolyte, a stable magnesium-sulfur cell could serve as a research platform for exploring the complicated multi-step reactions of the high-valence electrodes. However, applying practical conditions is also essential for demonstrating true advances in the electrochemical characteristics.[18,27,28]

3.4. Calcium-Sulfur Cells

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 54

In comparison to magnesium–sulfur battery chemistry that has entered its early research stage, the calcium–sulfur cell has just been demonstrated [31,248] as a new family member in alkalineearth metal–sulfur batteries. Moreover, as a new anode candidate for being coupled with a highcapacity sulfur cathode, calcium is the fifth most abundant element in the Earth’s crust, which suggests calcium–sulfur cells as a low-cost energy-storage system with a high energy density. However, the research on the relative battery chemistries and technologies is now restricted by several material and technical challenges.[31,244,308] To the best of our knowledge, only two articles in calcium-sulfur batteries have appeared in the literature, respectively, in 2013 as a primary battery and in 2018 as a rechargeable battery.[31,248,308b] In 2013, a primary calcium-sulfur cell was fabricated with a sulfur-mesoporous carbon nanocomposite as the cathode and a calcium foil as the anode. Ca(ClO4)2 was used as the calcium salt in an acetonitrile solvent, which could form a stable SEI layer on the reacted calcium and offer a high charge-storage capacity of 600 mA h g-1.[308b] However, the same SEI layer caused high impedance that limits the life-time and shelf life of the cell.[244,308b] Accordingly, a rechargeable calcium-sulfur cell would need an electrolyte that can plate/strip calcium metal with high Coulombic efficiency and a cell design that can attain high electrochemical utilization and stability with a sulfur cathode.[248,308] Recently, the concept of lithium-ion mediation has been proposed to make the calcium-sulfur cell rechargeable.[248] In the rechargeable calcium-sulfur cells, the lithium ions added in the electrolytes are the mediator for reactivating the redox products of the calcium-sulfur system and for facilitating ion diffusion in either the electrolyte or the SEI layer. Such bifunctions of lithiumion mediator allow the calcium-sulfur electrochemical reactions to have high reversibility, showing a high discharge capacity of 800 mA h g-1 and reversible cyclability for 20 cycles.[248]

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 55

Despite just entering the metal-sulfur battery system, the calcium-sulfur battery has displayed a great progress in achieving high electrochemical utilization and realizing electrochemical reversibility. The investigation provides a possible direction to develop reversible calcium-sulfur cells.[31,248,308b]

3.5. Aluminum-Sulfur Cells Aluminum metal has a high valence of 3+ and the highest density among the candidate metal-anodes that are proposed to meet high energy density demands (Figure 5a-d). An aluminummetal anode can provide three electrons per aluminum atom, which enables the cell to generate a theoretical gravimetric capacity of 2,980 mA h g-1 and a high theoretical volumetric capacity (8,046 mA h cm-3) — three-times that of lithium-metal (2,062 mA h cm-3).[29,30,309-312] Such a high theoretical volumetric capacity is also the highest value reported for metallic anodes thus far.[29,30,250] Aluminumsulfur cells can attain a high theoretical energy density of 1,300 W h kg-1 at a theoretical operating voltage of 1.23 V (Figure 5e-h).[250,311,312] Moreover, aluminum is the most abundant metal and third most abundant element found in the Earth’s crust (Figure 5e). Thus, batteries assembled with lowcost aluminum and sulfur would have a significant effect in terms of price.[244,250,309] The development of various aluminum-sulfur systems began in the 1980s. A non-aqueous aluminum-sulfur system was investigated by Marassi et al. in 1977,[313-315] while the aqueous aluminum-sulfur battery was initially proposed in 1993 by Licht et al.[316-319] Recently, Cohn et al. proposed the first aluminum-sulfur cell fabricated using an aluminum-metal anode and roomtemperature ionic liquid electrolyte of AlCl3 in 1-ethyl-3-methylimidazolium chloride and a sulfur/carbon composite cathode. This system can reach a high capacity of 1,600 mA h g-1 and shows

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 56

the potential to be electrochemically reversible.[309] A few follow-up studies have since begun improving the aluminum-sulfur cell performance with ionic liquid electrolyte.[18,29,30,311] This Progress Report will discuss aluminum-sulfur cells developed after 2015 and their electrochemical conversion battery chemistry (Figure 9 and Table 2).

3.4.1. Aluminum-Sulfur Electrochemistry Similar to lithium-sulfur and other metal-sulfur batteries, aluminum-sulfur cells generate electricity by the electrochemical conversion reactions among sulfur, polysulfides, and sulfides. The use of an aluminum-metal anode forms aluminum polysulfides with different chain lengths (Al2/3S8[309] and Al2/3S6[309,311]) during discharging, which end as aluminum sulfides (Al2S3).[30,31,309,311] The overall electrochemical reaction is expected to follow the conversion reaction: 3S + 2Al = Al2S3.[311,312] As a newly-developed metal-sulfur cell, there is not yet a generally accepted standard electrolyte system, nor standardized testing conditions. Thus, the detailed battery chemistry and reaction mechanisms are complicated and remain ambiguous.[18,29-31]

3.4.2. Aluminum-sulfur Electrochemical Performance Different studies have reported the challenges that impact the cell performance of the aluminum-sulfur system. The dissolution of sulfur-containing discharge species in the ionic liquid electrolyte significantly shortens the cycle life and damages the cycle stability.[309,311] The poor oxidation capability of AlSx species further causes aluminum-sulfur cells to demonstrate a fast capacity fade.[311] In contrast to the anode chemistries of other metals, which form the corresponding alkali-metal ions (i.e., Li+ and Na+), the discharge of the aluminum-metal anode forms

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 57

the negatively charged species Al2Cl7−. The larger ionic size of the Al2Cl7− species slows down the ion transfer.[29,30] Moreover, the dissociation reaction from Al2Cl7− to free aluminum ions has slow reaction kinetics.[310,311] These deteriorate the already slow kinetics of the cell reaction due to the use of the sulfur cathode, high-valence anode, and ionic liquid electrolyte.[30] The slow reaction kinetics may also impact the long-term cyclability and rate performance. Therefore, aluminum-sulfur cells normally demonstrate a high electrochemical utilization of sulfur approaching the theoretical capacity of sulfur in the initial cycle (Figure 9a). After that, the electrochemical instability leads to a fast capacity drop and low charge-discharge efficiency. The cycle life data reported so far is much lower than that of lithium-sulfur cells and other one-valence and two-valence metal-sulfur cells (Figure 9b). However, in the past 3 years, several aluminum-sulfur studies have proven the reversibility of rechargeable aluminum-sulfur cells and demonstrated the extension of the cycle life from 2 cycles to 50 cycles.[310-312] Moreover, although a low cycling rate is applied in cells to make up for the sluggish reaction kinetics, a newly-designed ionic-liquid electrolyte containing AlCl3 in N-butyl-N-methyl-piperidinium bromide achieved a fast 0.15C rate, which is much faster than the average cycling rate used in aluminum-sulfur research (0.03C rate, Figure 9c and Table 2).[312] The electrochemical efficiency and reversibility of aluminum-sulfur cells are not commonly reported. However, the use of ionic liquid electrolyte can avoid the formation of an irreversible surface passivation layer on the reaction aluminum metal so that a high Coulombic efficiency is expected.[311,320,321]

3.4.3. Performance Testing and Evaluation

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 58

Aluminum-sulfur battery reports have demonstrated the extended cycle life and improved rate performance of the system, though the amount of active material used and the volume of the ionic liquid electrolyte are basically unknown or regressed significantly as compared to the practical conditions developed in lithium-sulfur cells (Figure 9d,e and Table 2). The idealized conditions now applied in aluminum-sulfur studies have an average low sulfur content and loading of 50 wt.% and just 1.0 mg cm-2, respectively, which are among the worst cell assembly parameters used in any metal-sulfur system. These studies typically do not report the electrolyte volume or they use a high electrolyte/sulfur ratio. Such cell-design parameters will impact the electrochemical performance of aluminum-sulfur cells even more severely than other metal-sulfur systems as a result of the increased complexity of the conversion reactions.[46,59,76,90,210-212] In addition, the current progress in the cathode design and electrolyte formula has focused on limiting the dissolution of polysulfides in the electrolyte and improving the redox reaction of the active material.[29,210-214] These research directions are all closely related to the amount of active material and electrolyte in the cells. Therefore, it is essential to explore the battery chemistry and cell performance of aluminum-sulfur cells based on more practical cell-design parameters using a sufficient amount of active material and low enough electrolyte.[29,30] As a new member of the metal-sulfur system, aluminum-sulfur batteries have begun attracting more attention due to their potential to produce a high energy density at low cost.[309,312] However, as reports have shown, aluminum-sulfur batteries face significant difficulties in attaining high electrochemical utilization and reversibility. These electrochemical challenges result from the slow reaction kinetics between aluminum ions and sulfur during the charge-discharge processes.[249,250,312] One promising design for enhancing the reaction reversibility of aluminum-sulfur

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 59

cells was reported in 2018 with the use of a lithium-ion-mediated ionic liquid electrolyte that can improve both the cyclability and the electrochemical stability of the cell. The added lithium ions benefit the chemical reactivation of aluminum polysulfides and/or sulfide and, therefore, enable the cells to attain a high reversible capacity of 600 mA h g-1 after 50 cycles.[249]

4. Summary and Outlook 4.1. Current Progress and Future Needs in Lithium-Sulfur Technology We have summarized the current progress in lithium-sulfur cells in consideration of both academic findings and industrial technology. The number of lithium-sulfur publications has continuously increased over the last decade.[10,12,46,58,59] Over this time, the research community has built a systemic understanding of lithium-sulfur battery chemistry and the optimization of customized cell configurations and components.[12,45-47,66] The research direction has also been guided from the use of idealized conditions, which unfortunately result in overrated electrochemical performance and low energy densities. Attention needs to be focused on more practical conditions that will enable the community to address the real deficiencies in the electrochemical characteristics and feasibility of lithium-sulfur batteries.[10,12-16,58] Lithium-sulfur technology and cell assembly techniques are becoming more mature and practical, and some companies report being close to industrial production of the cells.[12-16,45-47,58-66] For instance, a number of companies have the manufacturing capability to produce large capacity lithium-sulfur cells featuring 400 Wh Kg-1 in gravimetric energy density.[58-65,322] Recently, analysts have forecasted the global lithium-sulfur battery market to grow at a compound annual growth rate of 71% during the period 2018 – 2022.[322]

However, lithium-sulfur batteries still have a lower

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 60

volumetric energy density than lithium-ion batteries due to the use of sulfur cathode, which naturally has a low density compared to the heavy lithium-insertion cathode materials.[10,46,66] In terms of future prospects, many analytical reports and review articles have noted that most studies and breakthroughs in the field have been achieved using coin cells under idealized conditions (i.e., low amount of sulfur with loadings of less than 4 mg cm-2 and contents of less than 60 wt.%, high electrolyte/sulfur ratios of over 11 μL mg-1, and excessive lithium metal). As we demonstrated in our discussion of lithium-sulfur technology, such conditions benefit the fast understanding of the basic battery chemistry and performance in conversion-type cells. However, continuously applying idealized conditions in lithium-sulfur cells might cause the electrochemical properties to be overrated and lead to low effective capacities and low practical energy densities. Specifically, operating at a relatively low discharge voltage of ~ 2.1 V, lithium-sulfur batteries need a high areal capacity of 7 mA h cm-2 in order to be comparable to current commercial lithiumion batteries, which offer 4 mA h cm-2 at 3.5 V. According to Table 1, to attain this capacity range, lithium-sulfur cells must deliver a practical discharge capacity of 995 mA h g-1. The reduced capacity output brought about by the sluggish reaction kinetics of the sulfur cathode means a relatively high sulfur loading of at least 7 mg cm-2 is necessary to compensate.[215,216] Besides attaing a high effective capacity, high energy density is another necessity for bringing lithium-sulfur cells into the future battery market. According to a perspective from the Toyota Research Institute,[58] lithium-sulfur cells have better cell performance in terms of gravimetric energy density as compared to volumetric energy density. The estimation of the energy density of lithium-sulfur batteries takes into account the whole device. Thus, a sufficiently high amount of sulfur of 5 – 7 mg cm-2 and 60 – 70 wt.% and low electrolyte/sulfur ratios of less than 5 – 6 μL mg-1

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 61

are necessary to meet a gravimetric energy density of over 200 – 400 W h kg−1.[15,58] However, due to the relatively low density of sulfur, lithium-sulfur cells currently cannot compete with present-day > 700 W h L-1 lithium-ion technology in terms of volumetric energy density. A strict balance of a sulfur loading of over 4 mg cm-2 with a sufficient sulfur content of 60 wt.% and a low electrolyte/sulfur ratio of less than 2 μL mg-1 is the bottom line for achieving a volumetric energy density of 500 W h L1 [12,15,58]

.

This implies that lithium-sulfur cells may be most appropriate for applications where

minimizing the mass is more important than the volume. Although high effective capacities and energy densities are now expectable in the future lithium-sulfur technology, high performances cannot be maintained over long periods of time as the cycle lives and stability of current lithium-sulfur cells are not competitive with the current-day lithium-ion technology. The cycle stability needs to be dramatically improved, which should be possible by using enough sulfur and acceptably low electrolyte/sulfur ratios.[12-16,45-47] Practical conditions are also necessary for improving our scientific understanding of the detailed conversion reactions in lithium-sulfur battery chemistry.[14,58-66] In terms of applications, the commercialization of lithium-sulfur batteries will take advantage of their low-cost, light-weight, and high-capacity. These features constitute major advantages for powering electric heavy-duty vehicles, unmanned aerial vehicles, high altitude aeronautical vehicles, and energy storage plants. For ground transportation, the abundant and lightweight cell construction materials and the high capacity of lithium-sulfur cells would allow buses and trucks to carry a considerably higher payload, while decreasing the operating cost and increasing the driving range. For aeronautical applications that consider weight as the utmost important parameter, the light-weight and high-capacity nature of lithium-sulfur cells could help keep the aviation devices

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 62

airborne for a longer time. For large-scale energy storage, the high-capacity lithium-sulfur cells could be coupled with conventional power plants for storing electrical energy when electrical production exceeds consumption, and for providing electrical energy when production falls below consumption. Based on the same concept, lithium-sulfur cells could also be linked with intermittent power plants (e.g., wind power, solar power, etc.) for utilizing renewable energy sources effectively and successively.[1-12,61-65,322] In the long run, other metal-sulfur cells might take over lithium-sulfur batteries because of the even lower cost of the cell construction materials; however, the heavier ions and/or higher valances could pose challenges for the reaction kinetics.[18-31,322]

4.2. Current Progress and Future Needs in Metal-Sulfur Systems As a follow-up battery technology developed from lithium-sulfur, the metal-sulfur batteries at this time struggle to be competitive with commercial lithium-ion and lithium-sulfur technology in many respects. However, significant breakthroughs occurred between 2011 and 2018, in which five major metal-sulfur cells have been reported with promising electrochemical reversibility and hypotheses in terms of the battery chemistry.[18-22,27-30,244] Current progress in the metal-sulfur cells has mainly focused on understanding the fundamental intrinsic material challenges. The main challenges that exist in the metal-sulfur batteries can be classified into two main categories: (1) the pre-existing challenges brought about by the sulfur cathodes and (2) new problems encountered in the metal-anode and electrolyte developments. Like lithium-sulfur cells, metal-sulfur battery cathodes also face challenges in effectively utilizing the sulfur and preventing its redox products from irreversibly migrating from the cathode

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 63

region toward the anode region. Although different metal anodes can be applied in the cell design, the insulating nature of sulfur continues to cause the sluggish reaction kinetics of the cathode.[10,35-37] With the use of metallic anodes with large ionic size and/or complex reaction mechanisms, the overall electrochemical efficiency and reversibility decreases further. Moreover, different polysulfide species can still form and dissolve into either the organic electrolytes used in sodium- and potassium-sulfur cells or the ionic liquid electrolytes used in magnesium- and aluminum-sulfur studies. The irreversible polysulfide relocation causes the same degradation problems in both electrodes and electrolyte. These intrinsic materials problems have inevitably drawn the research community into again applying idealized conditions in order to achieve better appearing, but ultimately overrated, performance, as demonstrated in the regression of all cell-assembly parameters.[18-22,46,66] On the metal anode side, metal-sulfur systems also need a desirable anode substrate and/or configuration to achieve high electrochemical reversibility and efficiency during the continuous plating and stripping processes of metallic anodes. Specifically, in the alkali-metal system, sodiumand potassium-sulfur cells inherit problems related to the strong reactivity brought about by the metal anodes. With a stronger reactivity than lithium, the sodium- and potassium-metal anodes face severer dendrite formation, anode/electrolyte instability, and material consumption.[18-22,27,28] On the other hand, the magnesium- and aluminum-metal anodes possess more charges and stronger interaction with the electrolyte. Thus, their solvation effect issues aggravate the sluggish reaction kinetics of the sulfur cathode, resulting in large polarization and poor electrochemical reversibility. For these reasons, it is essential to parallely develop effective electrolytes that allow the metal ions

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 64

to smoothly diffuse and ensure the redox reactions remain reversible, while preventing the fast migration of the cathode material, especially for multi-valent metal anodes.[19,22,25,29,30] In terms of future research prospects, the battery chemistry and development roadmap of lithium-sulfur batteries could be used as a basis for all other metal-sulfur cells. Although they operate differently, lithium-sulfur[12-16,18,45-47] and metal-sulfur batteries[18-22,27-30] are fabricated using a sulfur cathode and are based on conversion-reaction chemistry. [12-22,27-30] Specifically, it could be a shortcut to adopt what has been developed in lithium-sulfur batteries for metal-sulfur cells and then explore the corresponding electrochemical reactions. However, the idealized conditions that have been gradually abandoned by lithium-sulfur research are re-utilized again in current metal-sulfur studies, as shown in Figure 6 – 9 and Table 2. As a result, the metal-sulfur systems might take the same road as what lithium-sulfur has passed through over these past 10 – 15 years and suffer the same issues in transferring from idealized to practical conditions. Therefore, it is essential for researchers to apply practical conditions now to metal-sulfur cells to better understand the sluggish reaction kinetics, the electrolyte compatibility, and the metal-anode stability, which will determine whether or not the battery chemistry can be normally and reversibly operated, especially when the battery chemistry is complexed with a metallic anode featuring high reactivity or poor redox capability.

5. Conclusion This Progress Report collects and summarizes the developments in lithium-sulfur batteries and other metal-sulfur battery systems made with sodium, potassium, magnesium, calcium, and aluminum metal anodes. For each battery technology, we have explored the publication trends in

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 65

terms of the electrochemical materials, cell performances, cell-assembly parameters, and cell-testing conditions and drawn conclusions in terms of how they have affected the electrochemical efficiency and practical feasibility. The lithium-sulfur battery is the most mature system thus far and is expected to enter the battery market in the near future. Its battery chemistry and development history can serve as a guide for other metal-sulfur batteries to follow. However, as the research community makes more progress, we can expect more challenging issues to emerge from the complex conversion-type battery chemistries being exploited. Unsolved problems remain, including poor reliability of the metal-anode, irreversible polysulfide relocation, and sluggish reaction kinetics. More importantly, conducting research using practical conditions is still difficult, while building scientific understanding based on idealized conditions may prevent us from addressing the real issues. Newly-emerged metal-sulfur batteries will generally face the same problems encountered by lithium-sulfur batteries but may be even more severe and complicated. Although progress can be accompanied by regression, lithium-sulfur batteries and metal-sulfur cells do offer different highenergy-density choices with the possibility of low-cost production, which is promising for the commercial viability of the technology.

Biographies

Sheng-Heng Chung is currently a research associate at the Texas Materials Institute and the Materials Science and Engineering Graduate Program at the University of Texas at Austin. His research is focused on electrochemical conversion and storage materials.

This article is protected by copyright. All rights reserved. 1

Page 151

Professor Arumugam Manthiram is the Cockrell Family Regents Chair in Engineering and the Director of Texas Materials Institute and Materials Science and Engineering Program at the University of Texas at Austin. His research interests are in the area of materials for rechargeable batteries and fuel cells, including novel synthesis approaches for nanomaterials.

The lithium-sulfur battery is the most developed metal-sulfur system, which can serve as a guide for the development of other metal-sulfur batteries. Although progress is accompanied by regression, lithium- and metal-sulfur cells offer different high-energy-density choices with the possibility of lowcost production, which is appealing for commercial viability.

Keywords: lithium-sulfur batteries, metal-sulfur batteries, battery chemistry, cell fabrication, electrochemistry

Sheng-Heng Chung and Arumugam Manthiram*
